LADIES' LETTER

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

It would appear that some old fashions have teen revised this season that seemed likely to be out of date for many more years. It is said all styles come round and round in turn, only a certain number of years has to elapse before their revival. Who, for instance, would have thought we should so soon return to flounces? We have all been thoroughly comfortable and content with our plain skirts, than which nothing is prettier, yet all the same, the flounces have re-appeared. The Punahinello hats and bonnets are still to the fore, this shape now being made in felt and satin, usually turned up with a colour. A charming bonnet can be made in the flat shape of black astrachan, with just a bunch of violets on the left side, and the back trimmed with a large Alsatian bow of satin, from the centre of which emerges an osprey. Satin hats share a measure of our atten- tion, and occasionally those are to be met with a brim of straw, while murcrous-looking jot pins are thrust through black ostrich feathers ; and a prominent feature of oar millinery is the sable tail, bat this is rather stiff an'l unmanageable, and though it decorates with a certain amount of charm a brown felt plateau trimmed with a twist of tur- quoise blue velvet, and looks extremely well on a bonnet of white cloth embroidered in kt, yet it cannot be considered quite satisfying from an ?esthetic point of view.

A very ele2;ant bonnet is in two shades of velvet, and its appearance is very soft and becoming to the face. The crown is round and folded at the sides which are edged with Sequin jet or Venise lace, and soft plume of feather. It also has an aigrette on either side of the front.

Fars appear to be the order of the day. I sup- pe it is the sudden cold which has just now made its seasonable appearance that has induced us all to don our winter attire. Be that as it may, the streets in most towns have become suddenly peopled with fur-covered folk. These furs are many and various, but chinchilla is gaining rapid popularity, while beaver and astrachan trim mantles, jackets, and gowns indiscriminately. Fur, too, is being mixed with lace for evening dresses; hat, to be successful, these materials must both be of the richest quality, and, for this reason, such combinations must be left for the wealthy.

A very simple and yet stylish gown for girls of 13 or 16 is made with a round bodice finished with a narrow twist of shot silk to match the shaped revers and the full vest, which is arranged on the bodioe lining. The revers form a species of collar at the back, which is very effective in velvet, even with a silk vest, but velvet should be used for the twist at the waist. The gored skirt is simply trimmed with three bias tucks made up on muslin, and sewn on at the top edge, leaving the lower Adge loose and free. A costume for girls of 14 to 10, require five yards of material, but these measure- ments only allow for careful cutting, and do not admit of waste.

I have been enjoying a regular banquet of blouses and have seen then high, low, trimmed, untrimmed, in silk or satin, in velvet or watered silk, and in nun's veiling. There le indeed a brave array of this truly fascinating garment, and an improved edition of "The Nita" claims my first attention, the improvement consisting in the tucks being striped at inch intervals with cream-coloured insertion. For home evening wear in pale colours, for everyday wear in black, there is no model more entirely satisfying, for.it is guileless of any of the untidy looseness occasionally associated with a blouse, sad it is almost universally becoming.

The resdy.made ji.ckets and capes this year are really reinark4bly cheap and remarkably effective; they are trimmed for the most part with rows of braid at the waist and round the cape collars, and a narrow edging of fur makes iis appearance on those of a superior quality. Tee three-quarter lerigth jackets is the most generally adopted, and the tight-fitting coat finds fariur amongst the mullittide. A chic little cape for a young lady is Of nmoctli beaver cloth. The three bands of trim• ruing are brown velvet, also the collar, which is cut in a vary skilful manner. Yours lovingly, JENNIE.

Some of the healthiest children in the world are found in the Scottish Highlands, where shoes are seldom worn at an earlier age than 12 and 13. Th daughter born to Lord and Lady Dalkeith last week is the first grandchild of the Duke and Duchess of Buccl euch, and also of Lord and Lady Newport. Lady Charles Belesford's favourite holiday resort is bed ; her flower is the rose ; she likes the hottest period of the year best ; and her favourite pastimes are travel- ling and reading. There died recently in Louisville Miss Sallie Matthews who, although only thirty-three years old at the time of her death, had at one time commanded an Ohio River steamboat, employing one hundred men. The Princess of Wales is still suffering from the con- tinued melancholy which was brought about by the loss of her elder and favourite son, and which her long jour- neyings and stays on the Continent have been powerless to remove.

The Err-Tress of Russia is as slender and little as the Czar Is strong and massive. She is not beautiful, and not plain. Her features are well formed, and her eyes are bright and kindly. She is one of the most beautiful dancers in Russia, and is as fond of dancing as a country girl. Toe Princess Stephanie of Austria has just given her wedding dress to be made into vestments for the priests of the Church at Boyen. The dress was white brocade, embroidered with silver, and makes a gorgeous cope,and the lace is used for the altar frontal. The pattern of the brocade was the arms of Saxf-Coburg and Hapsburg, entwined with garlands of stephanotis. The Duke and Duchess of Fife are passionately fond of dogs, and when they travel between their various houses they always take a great number of special canine pets with them. The Duke is most fond of collies and Scotch terriers, the Duchess of fox terriers and pugs. When they are in London they may be often met on fine mornings in Hyde-park with half-a-dozen assorted dogs following at their heels. Sir Edwin Arnold, whe is now living in a flat, tells us that he never ties himself to any residence for more than six months at a stretch. He loves change. He has never reproved or chidden any of his children, and of his wives he frankly says have been married twice, and both the ladies who did me the honour to live with me (one for twelve and the other for twenty-one years) were ex- ceedingly happy and contented women."

Both the Princess Louise (Marchioness of Lorne) and Princess Beatrice have tried "wheeling." A couple of tricycles are kept at Balmoral for their use, and though they have never been outside the grounds of the palace, both Princesses frequently ride in the park. The are not good riders, but they use the cycles merely to give themselves some amount of muscular exercise.

Manly Amusements. Apropos of the manly amusements to which our girls ars now taking, the following amusing verses appeared lately in a London periodical : There's the girl who's stuck on fencing, and the girl . . .

who's just commencing to be somewhat interested' in the art of selt-defence ; There's the girl who's good at riding, and the girl who takes to striding over leagues of dale and mountain with energy intense ; There's the girl who worships rowing, and the one who's fond:of showing a marksmanship astounding in a

person of her sex ; There's the girl who's always ready, with a nerve both true and steady, when woeful dangers threaten or difficulties vex ; But despite the manly carriage, and the open scorn of marriage, which the little independent ladies seem to think so nice, You may perhaps have noted, that they're very seldom quoted,as having lost completely their inherent fear of mice.

How to Clarify Sugar or Syrup. There are many degrees of boiling sugar. Syrup must not boil over (it blazes), nor ho allowed to burn in the pan. The Ist degree is candy or thread, 2nd " souffl-," 3rd feathered. 4th crackling, and sth caramel. The last is an elegant cover for sweetmeats, but failures considered, is can begot as cheap from the confectioners as when made at borne. For ecmpotcs, i pints water to llb of sugar, boiled for hour, removing scum as it rises ; for preserves to keep, two parts double-refined loaf sugar and one of water, boiling for a little while. Clarify thus :—Dissolve 21bs. loaf sugar (not on the fire) in a pint of water ; add white of an egg, which well beat. Put into pan, stir with wooden spatula, and when syrup begins to swell and boil op throw in 2 tea- cupfuls of cold water. Don't stir it after this, but bring to boiling point again, then take off to settle ; also to remove all scum, Strain. For Ist degree put again over fire to boil until smooth, when dip a hand into cold water and the skimmer Into the sugar. Touch with sugar the thumb and forefinger, which open instantly. At 'first a fine short thread will form, a few minutes more and 'it willi ,be longer and stronger. Boil more for 2nd degree. Dip skimmer, blow sugar off and bubbles will form on the holes. For 3rd, boil further dip skimmer, shake it, and give sudden flirt; sugar les off at this stage like small feathers or down. For 4th degree, boil till on dipping a stick in and plunging it into cold water, the sugar snaps and becomes instantly hard. Tte above is from Mrs. Beeton's "Book of House- hold Management."

"Afternoon Naps."—By a Family Doctor. As I have already remarked, benefit chiefly accrues to the young and the old. Food is required by the young not only to carry on the ordinary functions of the body. but also to promote growth (which is very rapid at times, and may even outstrip the supply of material). It is obvious, therefore, that in these cases digestion ' should be as perfect and as rapid as possible if the body is to be maintained in good health and to grow normally. As the period of most rapid growth is comprised within the first four years of life, it is cf advantage to children, during this time at least, to sleep for a while after din- ner. We must not forget another consideration. All the bodily functions are capable of having regularity hn- pressed on them in a very marked degree, so that if the habit of regular and perfect digestion is formed in early life, it is likely to persist and add much to the happiness and success of after-life. Every influence which helps to form this habit ought, therefore, to be encouraged by those to whom the care of children is entrusted. In old age we have quite a different et of conditions to con- sider. The vital processes have now become enfeebled and the organs of the body meet, only with difficulty. the demands made upon them. Every help tt-iould therefore be given. not only by providing suitable food, but also by assisting the processes of digestion. The afternoon nap is Nature's way of doing the second duty, and it is of great advantage in promoting a healthy old age. We must also remember that in old age sleep at night is not so sound and regular as it is in earlier life; so, on the principle that every little helps, the afterno;,n nap adds to that period of daily rest which is de- mended by the body. It has been stated, with truth, that the habit may even tend to cause sounder sleep at night. Sleep begets sleep—especially in those who art- sleepless from overwork.—" Cassell's Family Magazine."

All her life she had been accustomed to give up to beautiful Uldene in all thmgs. Se loved this beautiful, spoiled, dark-eyed creature, whom she believed to be her sister, with a love that was almost idolatrous. She would have given her bright young life—had it been the price—to save Uldene's.

"You are happy in his love, Uldene ?" she said, wistfully; "let me hear you say so dear." "I am morn happy than I can tell you, Vezlis, she answerot raising those dark. starry eyes to the blue ones, so heavy with unshed tears. "I love him so well that if I were to lose him Ito would kill me, or I should lose my reason." From that moment heroic, noble Verlie put all thoughts of Rutledge Chester from her. What it cost her, only Heaven and the listening angels knew, who heard her pitiful prayers for strength and saw her despairing boars. Bat even then—even at the cost of her own misery—she was glad Uldene was happy; that thought was her one consolation in those bitter hours of anguish when she fought such pil iful battles with her own heart—to crush out the love that had built its altar there. How she longed to go away—to creep back to the shelter of the old lighthouse—fling herself on her mother's breast to die there. Oh, fatal was the hour in which she had drifted away from that lonely island home, oat in the great hard world, where oho had met the hero of her girlish day- dreams, handsome Rutledge Chester, and learned to love him. When Mrs. Chester was removed to her room and a physician summoned, it was discovered that she limed been stricken with a severe fit of apoplexy —partly paralysis, induced by some great mental shock.

"There was no hope," the doctor said, and advised them to send for Senator Chester without delay ; but this was impossible, for, only a few days previous, the benhtor had been called sud- denly abroad by a very important affair of the State, and it would be quite a week yet before ho reached port and found the cablegram awaiting him, apprising him of his wife's sudden and danger- ous illness.

If the worst came, all would be over before he oould possibly reach hmoe. WhenUideue heard of Mrs Chester's arrival, ha!f fainting with fear, she made her way to her room to beg her to keep her terrible secret. She was met with the startling news that when she had heard of her son's marriage she had dropped to the floor like one dead ;. and from that moment she had lost speech and action—that never again would those lips speak ; they were stricken dumb for ever. more. A terrible cry fell from Uldene's lips, for she knew whv—ah,, stir k -ew why.

Only Heaven could have told how the dying woman regretted, on her death-bed, that she had *ot made a confidant of her husband, that he, in turn, might have warned her idolized, handsome son, of the fatal barrier that lay between him and beautiful, hapless Uldene, the girl-bride whom fate had decreed must never love, for she must sever marry. During those hours Rutledge Chester sat by his mother's couch, he had imagined himsvlf alone with her; but such was not the mule. He was never Aunts with her! Behind the velvet hangings of the bed crouched a girlish figure, who listened, with bated breath, to every word that fell so musically from Rutledge Cheater's lips. It was Uldene—his bride. She knew what paper the dying mother longed so ardently to place in her son's hands. Uldene knew, too, that he would not find the paper— Mark Set ton's letter—in the safe; for at that moment it was hidden in her own bosom, against her own wildly throbbing heart. The situation was cruel. But in the hour of Mrs. Chester's death, Uldene cried out, in the secrecy of her own room, "that Heaven had spared her. If Rutledge'a mother had been given the power of speech, she would have divulged all, and that would have separated her from Rutledge for ever." "I will take him far away from here," she told herself—" so far away that Mark and Neils. Sefton can never find him to write him of the heavy curse that was predicted to fall upon my head on my eighteenth birthday. And he shall never know, and We will be happy. Yes, my great love will win like love from him in time, and earth can hold no greater heaven for me than that."

To that fortnight of bustle and Lonfuslon that; pased, Rutledge Chester had seen but little of Verlie. He knew that she avoided him whenever it was possible. He was thankful for that ; for in those dark, dreary hours he could not have re- sisted finding consolation with her, and breathing to her how lonely and desolate he was, had she been near.

At the end of the fortnight Verlie said good-bye to them, and started for home. She was glad to creep back to the lonely old light-house, as the storm-driven swallow is glad to seek its nest under the eaves. She came suddenly, unexpectedly, and un- heralded, upon Mark and Nella, as they sat before I the roaring wood fire one chilly spring evening. I They did not hear the grating of the little boat as it touched the sand, for the thundering, continuous wash of the waves outside. Some one glided like a ghost across the floor; a I slender form slipped down on her knees before them; and a piteous, sad, young voice breathed out sobbingly, as two great, blue-bells of eyes, ' swimming with tears, were raised to her mother's face: _ _

"Mother 1 father your Verlie has come back to you " Both sprang to their feet with joyous cries, but almost In the same breath they called for Uldene. "Bring the lass in, Nella !" cried the cheery old lighthouse keeper. "No doubt she is outside of the door, waiting to play some prank upon us, bless her pretty, roguish face ! "Come in, Uldene, lass 1" he roared, thumping his cane, while Nella flew to do his bidding. But Verlie called her back. I "She is not there, mother," she said, gently. "Uldene will never come to us again. She is a great lady now. Did you not receive the letter which she wrote to you, telling you that she had married Senator Chester's son, Rutledge ?" "Married I' shrieked Neils. Sefton, springing to her feet in the wildest agitation, while Mark grew pale as death. "My God I have I heard aright, child? Did you say Uldene—had—married?" "Yes, mamma," said Verlie, raising her blue eyes in wonder to her mother's frightened face. "I said she had married Rutledge Cheater." ( To be continued).

QUID PRO QUO.

A few years since, at one of our New Englana eolleges, the students had contracted such a habit of being out late at night that a law was put on requiring every student to be in at, or before, ten o'block in the evening, unless he should have per- mission for a later absence from one of the mas- ters; and the penalty, in case of infraction, was the remaining out all night, and being further dealt with in the morning. The porters who had charge of the outer doors through the night were instructed to allow no student to come in after the designated hour, under any pretence whatever, except the name had been given to them as that of one having permbssion to be absent to a later hour.

Well, on a certain evening Jack Windham, • merry-making, fun-loving, but really good and honest fellow, chanced to be half an hour late. He bad been in very pleasant company—the company of ladies—and the glittering momenta had passed unheeded. At half-past ten he knocked very gently at the outer door of the college dormitory. The old janitor of the institution happened to be on duty at the time—a work ho seldom performed ; but he had let the porters go away to an entertainment on this evening, and was sitting up in their gilt.. " Who's that 7" "It's I—Jack Windham. Is that you, Peabody?" " Yea." -

"Well, let me in. Come that's a good fellow." But the old janitor was stern. Ile could not break the laws of the college. At length, how ever, upon Jt.ek's pushing a silver dol.ar under the door' which Pe.l.)ody could reach and appro- priate, the door was carefully opened. The old fellow whispered to the student that he should make no nise, and would have pushed him to- wards the stairway, but Jack had forgotten some- thing. " Say ! Good old friend I I believe I've left a book on the window ledge outside. Will you slip out and get it for me ?" Without hesitation the old money-lover went out, and as soon as he had reached the step out- side Jack closed the door upon him and locked it. The poor janitor, not half dressod,w as shivering in the chill night air, and, speaking as handy ashe dared, he asked the student why he had looked him out. No answer.

" Jack ! Mr. Windham ! Will you let me in ! I am freezing ".. . _

" lie !i8 that you, Peabody? Ah ! you know what the law is, I N4'ould open the door if Idared, but the law forhids."

"0 ! nonsense? I opened the door for yon." "Did you ?" "Why, you know I did." "0 1-7-ah i—yes ! I remember me; and I gave you a silver dollar. My dear Peabody, present within mine optic range the same quid pro quo that I did give to thee, and in thou comeat. With a groan, the shivering janitor pushed the piece of 'silver under the door again, and was quickly thereafter admitted.

THE PURSER CALLS ON THE CONSTABLE.

- - - -- When the parser of one of the White Star Line of steamships started, one day in June, 1891, to call on his friend, the constable of St. John's Market, Liverpool, neither the purser noir the constable could have had the slightest tuition ivhat results woldd lollow the conversa- tion which was yet to'telte place between them. Who can predict when an idea will knock up against a fact? Nobody. Yet, when it does, the sparks are apt to 81 and something gets done. The steam rushed from the spout of the tea-kettle; Watt saw it, and the steam- engine was born. Constable Hughes needed an item of information, The purser possessed it, and what the result was you will have to read the following statement to find out.

[cony.] " I William Hughes, of 24, Fo wler•street, Breck-roads Liverpool, do solemnly and sincerely declare as follows : " For upwards of eight years I was market constable at St. John's Market, Liverpool, and always enjoyed excellent health up to the spring of last year (1890), when owing to irregularity of meals I fell into a low, weak state, and felt so languid and tired that I could not make cap whatever had come over we. I had changed from a strong, healthy, cheerful man to a weak, nervous low-spirited condition. My appetit- fell away, I had na relish for my meals, and what little I ate gave me dis- tress and filled me with pain at my sides. I had a dreadful pain in the small of my back as if I had bees stabbed.' Later on my eyes and skin were of a salloW colour, and my urine was very high coloured, and I threw up a quantity of thick phlogin. I ttrativally got weaker and weaker, and was only able to keep on with my work with difficulty. I struggled on as best I could. I lost a deal of sleep at night, for I would wake up at early morn, and could not get to sleep again, and when I got up in the morning I telt weak and tired. I lost all my former energy and spirit and felt so nervous and miserable, • as if isome- i hing was hanging over me? In the market I walked b..ait in a wretched way, for I was so nervous that; I shook all over role, and my bands shook •as if magne- tiscd.' In thisestate I continued until September, 1800, when I took cold, and I felt that my kidneys and heart were affected.. I went to a doctor, who said my ail' meat was produced by cold ; be gave me medicine, but I got no better. In the latter part of September, I noticed that with the phlegm I spat up blood, and this alarmed me, for fear my lungs were affected. As the blood-spitting did not cease, I went in November to a clever physician, who sounded me back and front, and told me my heart, kicineys, and lungs were all right ; but he said, • I would not give much for your liver.' I Look his physic for some time, but I got no better, and I began to despair of ever getting better again. I neat spent pound after poiind, trying all the remedies I could bear of, and I also, saw several chemists and doctors, but nothing gave me any real benefit. In January last I got so downhearted, and weak that I lost all heart and had to give up my employment, and I lingered in this wretched condition until June last, when a friend of mine, who is a purser on the White Sat Line of Steamers called at my house and told me of a medicine called Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup, and recommended it so highly, and begged me to give it a fair trial. I seof at once to Lewis's, in Ranelagh-street; and got a bottle of the Syrup, and commenced taking it, and in two day's f It easier, my food digested, and I enjoyed my food. After this the pain and languor left me, asd I soon re- gained my former good state of health, and have never felt better, in my life than I have done since. Alto- gether I took four bottles, and I thank Seigel's Syrup for my present good state of health. For the benefit of others whom it may concern, I give full permission to the proprietors of the medicine to make what use they think of this statement.

"And 1 make this solemn declaration conscientiously believing the same to be true, by virtue of the provi- sions ()The Statutory Declarations Act, 1835 (5 and 6 William IV., c. 62).

(Signed) William Hugbea. " Taken and declared by tbe.saicil

-William Hughes tit Liver- pool, aforesaid, this 11th day I of September,. 1891, before t me, W. Boyle, a Corn. ( missioner to administer Oaths in the Supreme Court of tiao. Judicature in England." In the name of the miserable multitude who suffer from indigestion and dyspepsia we beg to thank the purser,because thefour millions who live within a radios of thirty miles from Manchester, most of whom know where St. John's Market is, will read this incident, and hundreds will owe their restored health to it. Whoso- ever scatters useful knowledge is a benefactor to his kind. This the purser has done. We also congratulate Mr. Hughes on the fact that his friend called at 24, Fewler-st., just when he did. Men are apt to talk of errands as "important" or "not important." Who knows in advance what is important and what is not? No one on earth. We don't know why the purser Set out to look up the Constable that day, but wo risk the assertion that what he actually did was a thousand times more important than what he went to do.

Not a day passes that some incident does not arise to justify the distrust which the poor feel in the administration of the law. A case which has already been commented upon by some papers merits immediate notice from the Home Secretary. The victim is a poor man name.l Read, who was convicted of armed night poaching last week by a Northampton jury—another jury had disagreed— and sent to gaol for three months. Not a vestige of proof that Read and his companions were armed was offered. They were surpriseel by a farmer and his three sons, who were armed. Read was knocked down and held, while these peaceable agriculturists fired on the retreating poachers, badly wounding one man. Then Read was hauled before the magistrates and sent for trial to the as- sizes, with the result indicated.

The identity of the other men was known, but Read was victimised. If, as should have been the case, the man who fired on human beings under such circumstances had been ttied for attempted murder, Read would have been the most important witness. The administrators of "justice " not only declined to take steps to punish the real criminal, but promptly closured the witness ot hi • crime. And yet, there are men who fe.gn surprise because the rustics distrust the squires on the bench. In this instance, judges' justice went hand in hand with justices' justice. Three months was a scanda- lous sentence for such an offence. Read was poach- ing, no doubt ; but the safety of a rabbit is deemed a more sacred trust by the law than human life. Hr. Asquith ought to lose no time in becoming acquainted with the facts of this outrageous case.

It is again strongly rumoured that some of the Peers wil make an attempt when the Employer.' Liability Bill is before them to re.inAate in some form the doctrine of "common employment." This would, of course, mean a vital blow at the principle of the whole bill, and would be tantamount in effect, though not in form, to dividing on the second reading.

The Tories are still fishing for "a London pro- gramme" to put in opposition to that which ha. sent an overwhelming majority of Radicals to the County Council. 1 hear that the Duke of Norfolk is among those who declare that something must be done. The Duke, in fact, has gone so far as to admit that the present method of taxation in the capital is not satisfactory, and the Tory proposal will, it is certain, include some plan for stopping the immigration of destitute aliens. This last is a catching cry in the East End. '

A crisis has arisen in English Anarchism which is traceable to other influences than the refusal of the use of Trafalgar Square. The evidence ef this crisis is provided in the fact that a conference has been summoned, at which the exact position of the party will be defined. This conference is rendered all the more necessary inasmuch as the section which de- nounces the advocates of bomb-throwing is daily growing in strength. One of the purposes of the conference is to define the attitude of Anarchism towards Socialistic bodies, but an intimation has been received from leading Socialists that there can be no interchange of opinion so long as outrages of the Barcelona type are applauded. What, however, is of more moment to the Anarchists is that the members of the moderate section of the party have distinctly declared that they will no longer contribute towards the furds unless the wilder pro- paganda is abandoned. This means that a sece,sion is probable. In such an event a good deal of interest will attach to the attitude of Prince Kropotkin.

THE WARRINGTON CHAMBER OF COM:

MERCE. ,1 We have received the following communication from the Warrington Chamber of Commerce, which will be of interest to the local manufacturers : " It is much to he regretted that British merchants and manufacturers do not adequately exert themselves, like the German to fill the place vacated by France, in conse- quence of the existing commercial misunderstanding between the two countries, as purveyers of the very best class of goods to Switzerland. To effect this there should be some combination with the object of maintain- ing a commercial agent in Switzerland, who would, at fixed times, visitthe chief trade centres and fashionable health resorts with patterns and samples of British goods such as gentlemen's and ladies articles of dress, broad- cloths for officers' uniforms (that sent from Germany being inferior in quality), the hest English carpets, car- riage springs, and fittings of all kinds, boots and shoes H(adapting their shape to present requirements) and every kind of iron and leather article, of superior qual- ity with which Germany is unable to compete in excel- lence. To quote a case in point: Application was made recently to a person of high position in Berne for the , address in England of a manufacturer of bunting, by a person who supplies the Swiss Government with flags under contract, and had hitherto always obtained that material from France. Again the manager of a large boot and shoe shop in Berne, which was noted for its stock of British manufacture, recently informed a cus- tomer that he could no longer supply his family with the article, as the general fancy was still for painted toes and the new fashioned square ones sent him from Eng- land were as yet too little appreciated in Switzerland. This is another instance of the inability of British manu- facturers fully to comprehend the importance of adapting manufactures to the requirements of foreign consumers. The Chamber has also received particulars of a great International exhibition to be held in Hobart, Tasmania, in 1894-95. Any local firm who would care to exhibit will be supplied with full particulars on application to the secretary, Market Gate Chambers.

SENSATIONAL INCIDENTS IN DITBLIN,

A tin box with a fuse attached to it was discovered in the precincts of the Aklberough Barrack, Dublin on Sunday morning. On examination it was found that the fuse had been ignited but had gone out. The box contained a quanity of saturated materal and a cap of the detonator type, to which the fuse was attached. It has not up the present been ascertained whether the box was thrown over the wall into the barrack yard or placed Where it was found by a person inside. Early on Monday morning an incident occurred which, viewed in 1 the light of the foregoing statement, may be of some importance. About one o'clock a member of the Dublin police force on.duty in Sackville-street heard a sharp report in Tyrone Place, near the Roman Catholic Cathe- dral,and on going the spot found a burning fuse but no trace of explosive material. He subsequently arrested a man named Walter Sheridan, in whose pocket he found a box containing a number of detonators. Sheri- dan was charzed at the Northern Divisional Police Court on Monday afternoon with having an explosive in his possession in suspicious circumstances. He was allowed to give evidence on his own behalf, and stated that he had purchased the box of detonators from a man in a public-house on Saturday, net knowing what they were. The prisoner was remanded for a week. There was a sensational development of the fuse incident in Dublin on Monday night. A man named Patrick Reid. a bricklayer, who had been with Sheridan at the time of his arrest, was shot dead between seven and eight o'clock, in a dark and unfrequented locality. No arrest has been made. Later information states that the shooting of the man Patrick Reid in Cardiff-lane on Monday night caused a great sensation in Dublin when the facts of the occur- rence became known to the citizens on Tutsday morning. Taken in connection with the discovery of dynamite on Sunday last at the Aldborough Barracks, it is generally beliaved that the incident will furnish a clue to the police upon which they will be able to work out some solution of the series of explosive outrages that have taken place in the city during the past two years. No additional information is airailable as yet in reference to the occurrence, except that the police have been very active and have brought several persons to Dublin Castle for the purpose of subjecting them to inquiries. None of these parties were detained, and it is not known whether information of any character was elicited from them. As to the shooting of Reid, many theories are suggested, but the one which finds most favour is that the unfor- tunate man, haying apparently some knowledge of the secrets of the conspiracy, He urged by his companions to leave the country. He had in his possession when killed V, a sum which a man in his position would not be likely to have unless there were some special reasons for it, and he was pr.bably given the money to pay his passage. It would seem likely that it was in conse- quence of a reluctance to leave at the last moment that his friends resolved to do away with him. Nearly all the steamers for English and Scotch ports leave about seven o'clock, and ire was possibly on his return from the quay, having made up his mind not to go, that his friends accompanied him into the dark place where he was afterwards found dead.

Lord Randolph Churchill's incongruous combination of brown boots and silk hat annoys tho " County Gentle- man." "Why." it a,ka, " does he wear brown boots with a top hat ? It looks too shocking for words. I saw several exquisites nearly faint away when they encoun- tered this anachronism in dress. Someone really should tell his lordship that brown boots do not go with a silk hat." llorr Joseph ITollman, the distinguished violoncello player, was born at Maestricht forty-one years ago. A RAILWAY MAN'S ESCAPE.

(From the Crewe " Advertiser.") Crewe, the great railway town of the North of Eng- land, is one of the most extraordinary instances of large towns suddenly springing up owing to some special or fartuitous circumstance. A few months ago Coppen- hall, hitherto nothing but a sort of semi-rural suburb, was incorporated with the borough, and that is how it corn s tbout th tt Mr, Willkm Cornea, of 56, Bradfield- road, considers himself a native of Crewe. The reason for sending an " Advertiser" reporter to interview the gentleman above-named was to discover the truth or otherwise as to some rumours that had been going about with regard to what we might call an experience of suffering and miraculous cure through which he had gone. The reporter found him digging in his neat and well- kept back garden, for, owing to the eoal strike, the rail- way works were OR short time, and this was one of his enforced and unwelcome holidays. On being told the nature of the reporter's errand, Mr. Cornea very soon grew eloquent upon his sufferings. In October, 1892, he was attacked by a severe cold, which in the following spring developed into acute rheumatism in his leg and hip. As he himself feelingly said : " It was so bad that I couldn't sit straight in my chair for five minutes together ; and after I had got a little sleep, the pain of shifting in bed or trying to get up in the morning was really excruciating." "Did you go to work ?" "Yes, I managed to hobble backwards and forwards to the locomotive shop where I work, but I was in aw- ful agony. I couldn't stoop, and when I went to wash myself, I couldn't even bend down to the basin." " And you are quite well now ?" " Quite," arid ho gave an expressive glance towards the potatoe bed he had been digging, as much as to say "That doesn't look like rheumatism, does it ?" Well, Mr. Cornea, go on with your interesting story, please." " I tried everything—doctor's stuff, patent medicine of all sorts, and those little common remedies, such as turpentine, that my friends told me about, but nothing touched it, even for five minutes. One night, however, I was noting the' Liverpool Co urier," when I came across a remarkable case cured by Dr. Williams' Pink , Pills for Pale People, and I sent off at once for a box. " Well, I hadn't taken one box before I felt better,and by the time I had got through three,tny pain had all dis- appeared, and I could walk, stoop, and dig. But it did more for me than even that." " More still !" .

" Yes. I had been deaf for nigh on twelve years, but when I had taken two boxes of these pills, to my great astonishment and delight, I began to hear plainly, and by the time four boxes had gone, my deafness had al- most quite gone too." " And you attribute all this to the pills ?" "Why, what else could it have been ? It was them sure enough." Miss Elizabeth Beecher, an adopted thughter of Comes came in in the middle of her Saturday cleaning up and corroborated all that her father had said. Indeed she was even more enthusiastic than Mr. Cornea himself on the chanoe for the better the pills had worked in his appearance and in his health. It was painful, she said, to see him moving about, and nothing seemed to have the slightest effect until he heard of these pills, which worked a change nothing short of miraculous. " And you think, Miss Beecher, that these pills cured him." •

"I only know we tried almost everything under the sun but nothing touched the pain until he began these pills, when it went with marvellous quickness." Thanking Mr. Comes and his adopted daughter for their courtesy, the reporter left, deeply impressed with their story. Dr. Williams' Pink Pals are a positive and unfailing ' cure from all diseases arising from an impoverished state of the blood,such as anwinia, pale and sallow com- plexion, green sickness, general muscular weakness, de- pression of spirits, loss of appetite, palpitation of the heart, shortness of breath, pains in the back, nervous headache, loss of memory, early decay, all forms of female weakness, hysteria, paralysis, locomotor ataxy, rheumatism, and sciatica; else of all diseases arising from vitiated humours in the blood, which cause scro- fula, rickets, hip joint diseases, chronic erysipelas, con- sumption of the bowels and lungs, and that they invi- gorate the blood and system when broken down by over work and worry, diseases, excesses and ,indiscretions of living. These Pills are not a purgative medicine. They contain nothing that could imjure the most delicate sys- tem. They act directly on the blood, supplying to the latter its life-giving qualities, by assisting it to absorb oxygen, the great supporter of all organic life. In this way, the blood becoming "built up," and becoming supplied with its lacking constituents, becomes rich and .1 red, and nourishfs the various organs, stimulating them to activity in the performance of their functions, and thus eliminating diseases from the system. BRITISH WORKMAN'S AND GENERAL

ASSURANCE COMPANY.

PRESENTATION TO MR. H. PORT.

The splendid reception accorded to Mr. H. Port. the Founder and Managing Director of the British Work- men's and General Assurance Company, at the Cannon- street Hotel, London, will long be regarded as one of the most noteworthy features in the Insurance History of the present year. The testhnonial presented to Mr. Port proves how highly he is esteemed by his confreres, and:it must have been particularly gratifying to him to find that after his long and anxious years of service, his great ability and merits have been widely acknowledged. The presentation was made by Mr. Sessions, the Inspec- tor of the Southern Division. the occasion of the gather- ing at Cannon-street Hotel being to bring the Company's representatives belonging to the London and South of England Divisions in touch with each other, BO that their efforts might be stimulated. Mr. Port's reply was in every way worthy of him, and he was repeatedly greeted with enthusiastic cheering. The great success ot the Company is unquestionably owing to Mr. Port's consummate tact and indomitable perseverance. Mr. Port believes in leading men, not driving them. As to the present position bf the " British Workman's," we find that it has considerably more than half a million of assurants. It has paid in Claims over £1,200,000, and the Annual Income exceeds £330,000. About ten years ago, it was only between seventy and eighty thou- sand pounds. It will thus be seen that the progress of the Company has been by leaps and bounds. Another strong point in favour of the " British Workman's " is that it is most merciful and considerate to its poorer members. Last year the Company gave back nearly six thousand pounds ! The recent gathering at the Can- non-st. Hotel will no doubt stimulate the agents of the Company to renewed exertions. And it will not only do this, but be a lasting and convincing proof with what affection and veneration the Managing Director of the Company is held by its officials, and by the insurance world generally. From a comparatively insignificant position, Mr. Port has raised the 6' British Workmen's into the very front rank of the industrial offices. He was the Founder of the Company, and be has every rea- son to be proud of the great work he has achieved.

Mr. Sims Reeves receives thirty shillings per hour for teaching at the Guildhall School. This is in additiou to the fees he receives from pupils.

WARRINGTON SOCIAL QUESTIONS

UNION.

FIRST ANNUAL MEETING.

The first annual IX eeting of the Warrington Social Questions Union was held at the Young Men's Christian Association Rooms, on Monday evening. Tho Rev. John Yonge presided. There was a large attendance of members.

The Rev. W. H. DkIIMMOND apologized for the absence of the Rev. J. W. Varley (secretary), who, he said, was ill in bed with influenza. Mr. Varley had forwarded hie books to him. Mr. Robert Garnett, Miss Dutton and Mr. John Gandv were also unavoidably absent. In Mr. Varley's absence be would present a short statement of the work which had been done by the union. This was called the first annual meeting, though, as a matter of fact, the union had not been ia existence a year. It was thought convenient to make November the time for hllding the annual meeting; indeed by the rules they had adopted they were compelled to hold it in that month.

TEE ANNUAL REPORT Op THE GENERAL PURPOSES COMMITTEE. The Rev. W. H. DRUMMOND then read the report of 1 the General Purposes Committee as follows :—" The Warrington Social Questions Union sprang out of a desire to take corporate action for the moral and social improvement of the town, irrespective of religions or political part). Sensible as the promoters of the union were of the valuable work done by the existing agencies in our midst, they were as far as possible from wishing t) interfere with them, either by the attractien a, new society might offer, or by attempting to do work which others have long done with great efficiency. But they felt the pressure of common evils calling for a more strenuous expression of common moral convictions. Corporate feeling and action are least of all to be des- pised when the object in view is the training of the public conscience, the raising of the public ideal, and the effective use of such remedies for public Ws as have already obtained the sanction of the Legislature. It cannot be too clearly stated that the union exists only to promote objects which must be cherished by every worthy citizen, righteousness, good-will, and the possi• bility of a good life for all. It may be asked what has the union done ? What is it going to do ? The first question must be answered simply by the statement that it has only been in existence for a few months, and few people will surely be found to cavil at its wisdom in devoting itself so far mainly to the work of quiet investi- igation. To the second question, what is it goingto do? only one reply is possible. It depends entirely upon the citizens of Warrington. It is theirs—a civic institution —to fashion into a noble instrument of good if they choose to do so. Perhaps its first and paramount duty is to devote itself to the education of the public con- science and the training of an intelligent public opinion on social questions. In this task it asks fer the hearty co-operation of all, and especially of those whose larger privileges of culture and education seem to lay upon them a special duty in this respect towards the commu- nity in which they live. In these first tentative months two public meetings have been held under the auspices of the union, on May let in the Lecture Room of the Museum, when the chair was occupied by the Rev. J. Yonge, and the names of several members were received and on November 13th, in the Co-operative Hall, under the presidency of Mr. Monks, J.P., when addresses were delivered by the Revs. J. Yonge, R. A. Armstrong, and C. F. Aked, of Liverpool. Other meetings with a view to arousing public attention to the objects of the union are contemplated. The work and aims of the various committees may be judged from their reports, which are appended." (Applause.)

TEMPEINCE COMMITTER. Mr. DRUMMOND then read extracts from the reports of the various committees. He remarked that the Temper- ance Committee spoke with pleasure of the manner in which the backdoor question was successfully brought forward at the Brewster Sessions. They spoke of the need of vigilance with regard to theatrical licences. The report ended by deploring the accession of strength to the liquor interest at the last municipal elections.

GAMBLING COMMITTEE. The Anti-Gambling Committee had been largely em- ployed in collecting facts, which revealed the fact that the evils of gambling were very widespread in their midst. The matter would be further considered, when the facts had been sufficiently collected and the union was clear as to the action which should be taken.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
SOCIAL PURITY COMMITTER.

The Social Purity Committee had mainly devoted itself to accumulating facts concerning inner morality, with the result that the committee were convinced that there was a great deal of difficult work before them. One of the first actions of the committee was to invite the co- operation of a number of ladies,who had formed a com- mittee to act in conjunction with the Social Purity Com- mittee, and they would doubtless be able to do a great deal of valuable preventative work. A series of dinner hour meetings for women and girls engaged in workshops and factories bad been commenced.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0032.txt | included=true | included =====
HOME LIFE commirrag.

The Condition of Home Lite Committee had also been employed mainly in collecting facts with regard to the housing of the poor in the town ; and as they would seo, that was a matter that required a considerable amount of time and attention. Before any public action could be takers it was necessary for them to accumulate a great many facts, and have the case clearly put before them.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
LAI3OI7R COMMITTEE.

The Labour Committee presented a somewhat lengthy report. The Secretary had informed him that the com- mittee applied to the Labour Department of the Board of Trade for a free copy of the " Labour Gazette " for the year, to the committee, and it had been granted. A series of the first Acts of Parliament governing the con- ditions of labour had been secured for the use of the committee. A movement of some importance affecting the shop assistants' half-holiday was brought under the notice of the committee, and it was decided to request I an interview from a deputation of the Shop Assistants' Association, which was granted. It took place on the 21st of July, and it was decided that the committee should help the shop assistants as far as possible in ob- taining the half-holiday. But, unfortunately, came a great period of distress,and it had not been possible to do anything further in the matter, but it would not be lost sight of, as the committee regarded it as A great social movement. The report spoke of the efforts to eAablish a labour bureau. So far nothing definite had been effected, but the committee reported that they hoped to be successful in establishing a labour bureau in Warrington.

EDUCATION AND RECREATION COMMITTEE.

11 The report of the Educational and Recreative Com- mittee said that the dulne,s of hie in many of the close and thickly populated districts was answerable for heavy drinking and other forms of wrong-doing. One step had been taken in the direction of providing healthy recreation by the inauguration of a series of Saturday evening concerts. They had been a marked succe&s. Thanks were duo to Mr.Stringer,and the Rev.J. W.Varley for the zeal with which they had thrown them- selves into the movenaent. The committee had ap- proached the committee of the Infirmary and the mem- bers of the Board of Guardians with a view to provid- ing newspapers and other suitable reading for the patients at the Infirmary and the Workhouse. Thee.", Mr. Drummond remarked, were the summaries of the reports of the various committees. It did not appear that a great deal of work had been accomplished, hut he thought what he had read showed them the sort of work which the union had in view, and was endeavouring to do. The union was wise in thus attempting, first of all, to make sure of its facts. All staple social reforms must be based up.m a reliable knowledge of social evils. It might be that for some few months in many depart- ments they would have to devote themselves to the study of social evils and social f tots as they existed in their midst. From that study social activity would re- sult. (Applause.) The CHAIRMAN addressed the meeting, and contended that those who criticized the movement would criti- cize it unfairly if they expected the union to present a statement showing that them had been achievements, or if they expected them to lay before the town an elabor- ate programme in detail as to what they intended to ac- complish. (Hear, hear.) As a whole, the Social Ques- tions Union had justified its existence, and he trusted that in the days and years to come it would do more to justify itself for having been called into existence. (Applause.) Whilst they were conscious that they had to face many stubborn difficulties, he should like , to say to them " Go forward with the movement with a confident heart." (Applause.) They believed ' they had right on their side, and that they were advancing according to the lead of the present times. In nearly all the principal towns in England movements of this character had been inaugurated, and were doing a practical work. Mr. Yonge thought the Corporation should take up the question of the erection of model lodgings for strangers passing through the town, and he also referred to several matters which might be advantageously taken up by the union. They should also take into consideration the desirability of ' circulating amongst the more intelligent part of the people pamphlets and literature on social questions generally. They ought to strengthen the hands of the police authorities by keeping the public informed of the I need there was for their action, and particularly of what had been done by the police in other towns. Um bad been very much struck (in reading the newspapers with a little more care than he had been in the habit of doing) with the fact that the police in other towns did not ap- pear to find the same difficulty in proceeding in thar j direction that the police authorities in their own trwn apparently discovered. (Hear, bear, and applause.) He saw that betting houses could be dealt with without nifficulty, were dealt with and suppressed and persons fined. Public-house betting was discoverable, provable and punishable, and if only these facts could be collated. and kept before the public eye, lie felt convinced that they would by degrees strengthen public opinion so much as to lead their authorities to see that it was desir- able for them of their own initiation to take some steps lit ate, direction be had named. (Applause.) He bad

much pleasure in moving that the reports be adopted and printed. Mr. MONII9, J.P., britily seconded the motion, which was carried.

DEBATE AT WIDNES LIBERAL CLUB.

The question of "Is female suffrage in Parlia- mentary elections desirable ?" was debated at Widnes Liberal Club on Monday evening. Mr.stewart Deacon presided. Mrs. Stewart Brown took the lead in the affirmative and argued that women's suffrage was in accordance with Liberal principles, and that there was a strong desire amongst women to be enfranchised. Especially was this the case amongst the working classes, so that the injustice of unequal wages paid to women, as compared with men for doing the same work might be done away with. This was not a modern need she was advocating, but an ancient right. Up to 1832 women had a vote in Parliamentary matters. She denied that the women were represented by the men, they were vers often misrepresented by the men. The marriage laws, divorce laws, and the laws relating to the custody of children were unjust to women. It was argued by the opponents of woman's suffrage that if the women were enfranchised they would out-vote the men. This was only a bogie held up to frighten them, for it was not at all likely that the women would vote unitedly on any subject. Men possessed a vote simply because of property qualifications, and votes should not be with- held from women simply because they had the misfor- tune to be born women. (Applause.)—Dr. Donnolley replying in the negative said he was almost persuaded by Mrs. Brown's eloquence to adopt the same platform. (Laughter.) It was argued that women had votes in connection with School Boards, Boards of Guar- dians, Itc., and therefore ought to have a Parlia- mentary vote, but a Parliamentary vote was ' quite a different character. Why did not women maintain the suffrage when it was given them ? There were so few women exercised their vete& that the law was removed from the Statute Book. Dr. Dontiolly strongly urged that woman's proper place was the house. If in these days of progress that, sphere was found too narrow he thought they might find plenty of work in the world around where squalor and vice existed, which by kindness and tenderness they could do a great deal to alleviate. (Applause.)—Mrs. O'Keeffe said women seemed to be quite good enough to go out at an election time to canvass for a candidate, and she maintained they had a right to vote.—Mr. H. Wade Deacon said Mrs. O'Keeffe had hit him rather hard about women only being good enough at election time to canvass (laughter), and it was with a great deal of diffidence that he opposed women's suffrage. If they begun, they must go on to universal suffrage, which was not at all desirable. Women's suffrage meant nothing short of a revolution.— Mr. Eustace Carey and Mr. Fred Hartland supported the affirmative. Mrs. Brown closed the debate with an effective speech, and a division was taken. The affirma- tive was defeated by four votes.

TIIR SCARLET FEVER OUTBREAK. SCHOOLS TO BE CLOSED.

Dr. Adams medical officer, reported ten fresh cases of scarlet fever at Kingsley and there was one fresh case at Newton by Frodsham, traced to Kingsley School. Measles had broken out at Norley. In view of the spread of these diseases in connection with Norley and Kingsley schools, he would suggest that the schools he closed till after the Christmas Holidays. A case of typhoid fever had occurred at Dutton. It was believed the diseaoe was contractPd away from home. The water however should be analysed, as typhoid fever occurred their last year. A case of scarlet fever had occurred at Harting- ton. The disease was contracted at Weaverham Schools, where he was inlormed the disease was prevalent. Nine- teen fresh cases of infectious disease had been reported since the last meeting.—Mr. Woodward moved that the schools at Kngsley,Norley,and Five Crosses be closed at once until after the Christmas Holidays and that the Christmas Holidays be as prolonged as possible. He also suggested that Sunday School Managers should have notice to cicse their schools.—Mr. Heywood seconded the motion.—Mr. Woodward said the medical officer had not notice of all the cases of scarlet fever in Kingsley. Dr. Adims : lain sure I havb not.—The motion was carried. A GUTTER MERCHANT ON HIS CALLING.

"Matches, pipe-cleaners, tie-clips, and lead-pencil sharpeners is wot we calls realer stock, and these all goes Pretty well if yer've got a decent pitch; but yer have ter 'it on a special line if yer wants to make a bit extry. Them there studs as wot don't want no pushin', hut tips on one side to go through the button hole, went very well when they fust come out, but lately I ain't done better with anything than I did with the penny sewing-machine all made ter work,' as come out a few months ago. Chem penny toys, too, wot Ter blows through and the figure er Mr. Gladstone starts choppin' down a tree, went very well when they was a novelty. I did a good thing, too, last year in Ch ,apside with them little penny terbacker pouches 'oldie just about enuf for one cigarette, an' it was me as christened 'em 'The Strong Man's Terbaclp'r Pouch,' though the others was goick enuf in catchin' up the name. Alt ! that's the spoilin' of the whole atie. There's too many of us at it, and any poor joker down on 'is lock reckons he's clever emit to sell pen n'orths in the gutter ;though, mind ver, there's room for a man's being smart at his job even there."—" Cassell's Saturday Journal."

THE WARDS QUESTION AT NEWTON.

The following correspondenCa will be of interest to residents in Newton and Earlestown : Newton Park, Newton-le-Willows, 30th November, 1893. Sir,—Being desirous that the ratepayers of the Newton township may be fully acquainted with all the ' proceedings of the Committee which has been formed in connection with the above, may I ask the favour of the publication of the enclosed correspondence, and thus enable the ratepayers to obtain a clear view of the situa- tion.—l am, yours truly, ROBERT STONE.

Newton-iwalakerfield Improvement Commissioners,- 2, Cairo-street. Warrington, 17th November. 1893.— Dear Mr. Stone,—' am directed by the Commissioners to send you a copy, which I now enclose, of the resolution passed at the Commissioners special meeting held yester- day to consider the question of the division of the dis- trict into wards brought before the Commissioners by the Deputation to the Board on the sixth instant.—lam, yours faithfully, JAMES E. WORSLEY.—Robert Stone, Esq., Newton Park.—Resolution ; "That in view of the Parish Conncils Bill not having passed through Com- mittee it is not advisable for the Commissioners to take any steps at present as to the division of the township into wards, until the effect of the Bill upon the Commis- sioners district is known." Newton Park,—Newton-le-Willows, 22nd November, 1893.—Dear Mr. Worsley,—l beg to acknowledge the receipt of your letter of the 17th inst. with a copy of resolution passed by the Commissioners on the previous day, and lam sorry they do not think it advisable at present to deal with the question of the division of the townships into wards. It must be conceded that the mode of representation to the Board we have asked the Commissioners to adopt,is right in principle ; it is there- fore to be regretted that the townships should not have the ipenefit of it at once, instead of having to wait a very Indefinite time.—Yours very truly, ROBERT STONE.— James E. Worsley,Esq.,Law Clerk Newton Improvement Commissioners.

Newton Park, Newton-le-Willows, 20th, November, 1893.—C. B. F. Borron, Esq., C.C., Wargrave.—Dear Sir,—As Chairman of a public meeting of the ratepayers of the Newton District of this township. I have been requested to communicate with you and obtain your views as to the course to be pursued to obtain electoral wards. I may state we have approached the Improve- ment Commissioners who have declined to take any steps at present. As you may be aware this body is appointed under a Special Local Act obtained in 1855, whereby the Board was to consist of ten members. The desire of the meeting was that the township should be divided into wards, an equal number of representatives elected from each ward and the number et metnbers to the Board increased, and the Meeting was of opinion that the County Council can assist them in this matter, We therefore shall be glad if you will kindly give this yeur careful consideration and opinion, at your earliest convenience.—l am, yours very truly, Ronzaz STONE. Wargrave, Newton-le-Willows, 25th November. 1893. —Dear sir,—Having considered your letter of 20th inst. very carefully, I have in reply to confirm what I have already mentioned to you, namely,that Section 57 of the Local Government Act, 1888,enables the County Coaneil to receive any application tor the division of an Urban Sanitary Authority into wards, and for the increase of its members. On the receipt of the application the mat- ter will he referred to the Parliamentary Committee,who will hold the necessary public enquiry before any ordut is made by the County Council. If you require any further information I am at your command.—Yours faithfully, C. B. F. BORRON.—Robert Stone, Esq., J.P., Newton Park, Newton-le-Willows. Newton Park, Newton-le-Willows, 28th November, 1893.—C. B. F. Borrom, Esq., C.C., Wargrave.—Dear Sir,—l beg to thank you for your letter of the 25th inst, stating the course now open for obtaining the object my Committee have in view. I may met Con, that although you had before indicated to me this method of proce- dure, my Committee had no desire to take any action without first approaching the Commissioners, in the ex- pectation that, after having laid our grievances before them, they would take the needful steps for their being remedied. In this, however, we have been disappointed, and at a meeting held last night, my Committee had de- cided to rely up..n the Local Government Act of 1888, and apply to the County Council. They desired use to express the hope that you will give them every assist- ance in the matter.—l am, yours very truly, ROBERT STONE. . . .

Newton Park, Newton-le-Willows, 28th November, 1.893.—J. W. Emmett, Esq., Chairman, Newton Improve- ment Commis•ioners.—Dear sir,—Division of the Town- ship into Wards.—The resolution of the Commissioners forwarded to me on the 17th inst. by the Law Clerk, was laid before the Committee which has been formed in connection with the above subject, at a meeting held last night. It was not considered a satisfactory answer to the prayer of the deputation which waited upon your Board on the 6th inst. and it is my duty to inform you that a resolution was passed by the Committee, " That the County Gmncil be petitioned to divide the township into wards, with an equal number of representatives to each, and to increase the number of Members to the Board."—l am, yours truly, ROBERT STONE.

SOME DARING ESCAPES

The sensational stories ot prison-breaking in which novelists delight are rivalled by actual escapes by verit- able prisoners. For inwance, not lorg ago a marvellous flight was planned and carried out by a primer la Carnarvon Gaol, whose cell was at the very top of the building. He made a large bole en the brick ceiling of his cell by miens of a loose plank, cereverted his bed- clothes into a rope, and wound the coil about his body. lie then clambered through the hole, and found himself under the prison roof. Groping his way in the dark- ness, her found a fastenial trip-door, forced it open, and emerged on the roof. Here the home-made ropo came into use, enabling the daring prison-breaker tee drop down to a lower roof, whence he slid by means of rain-water pipes to the outer wall of (he prison. Jump- ing in safety to the ground, he was fro. His next step was to enter a private house, and ' borrow ' a suit of clothes. Dunning these, ilf3 made off across thee country, and appears never to have been re-captured after his marvellous escape. This feat was almost equalled by a oar-armed boy of thirteen, wk., was shut up on the top floor of a London workaouse to await punishment for stealing a cabman's whip. Squeezing himself through the bars which guarded the window, the plucky lad managed to get hold of a ventilating shaft which ran up the side of the building, just within reach. Down thee shaft he clambered, dispite his terribly maimed state,tor about thirty feei—whon hee missed his hold, seed fell heavily es the ground. fie was not much hurt by his twelve-foot fall, but he was picked up by the workhouse porter, and so his daring attempt proved useless. But think of trusting yourself, with only one hand to hold by, to a smooth si aft four. teen yards from the ground ! A more desperate struggle for liberty was made some years ago by two Russian political prisonars—o is of them an F.nglishma.n by descent. Removing the flagstenes of their cell— which wets on the ground floor--( hey set to work every da k night, tunnelling out the earth beneath, secreting the soil removed in their pockets and trousers-legs, and replacing the stows before dawn. This went on for a couple of months, until the burrow extended right under the prison wall and almost reached the surface of the ground bey lnd. Then the workers awaited a favoura ble night. It came—dark, stormy, and cloudy. The heroic prisoner, burst through the remaining shed of earth,and stood outside the pr.son at last. Alas ! they were seen and fired upon by the guard. The Eiglishinan was killed, and his corn/melee wounded. re-captured and. sent to the Siberian mines—" chums.'

7116 ASYLUM.

Mr. Hornby, clerk of the Asylum Committee, wrote informing the Board that there was new a number of vacant beds on each side of the House, and it would not be necessary to make inquiry as to accommodation be- fore sending a patient. TRAMPS. The Shifnal Union forwarded the following resolution for approval : "That this Board begs to call the attention of the Local Government Board to the alarming increase of tramps throughout the country, and suggested that each tramp should be inspected by the police before admissien to the workhouse. Also that each tramp should carry a tiok‘t showing his identity and the place from which he has come, with his deatination, such card to be endorsed by the police constable, on presentation to him."—Tne communication was laid on the table.

RUNCORN HOSPITAL CHARCIRS. Acting under the instruction of the Health Committee Mr. E. Marshall, secretary to Runcorn Board of Com- missioners, wrote stating that whatever difference of opinion may exist as to the obligation they were under to pay for the maintenance of destitute persons in Runcorn Hospital there could be none in respect to five persons in receipt of parish relief, who clearly came within the terms of the Commissioners agreement with the Guar- dians. He therefore requested payment of the enclosed account amounting to £23 183. 6d.—The Guardians ! denied their liability and declined to pay the account. WIMP AT RUNCORN. Mr. Cooke, relieving iifficer, for Runcorn District, re- ported that through the kindness and generosity of the United Alkali Co., and other employers of labour in the district, with the assistance of a central relief committee large numbers of destitute families had been saved from troubling the Board. Thy had done much in he/ping to alleviate the distress of the past 18 or 17 weeks caused by the coal strike. Thereby hundreds of families had been relieved from week to week without being pail- perised and without burdening the rates. He was sure ! the Board would be pleased to hear of this most geher- °us munificence.—Approval of the efforts of the parties named to allevi:Ve the distress in Runcorn, was ex- pressed by several members. TIM PARISH COUNCILS BILL. The Committee appointed to consider the Local Gov- ernment (England and Wales) Bill, reported that they had held a meeting at Mr. Ashton's Frodtham office, on the previous night and they submitted for approval a number ef alterations to the Bill, and on the motion of Mr. Heywood it was resolved to forward copies to the members of Parlisment representing constituencies in the Union and ask them to support them in the House of Commons.—The motion was carried. CHARGE OF NEGLECT OF CHILDREN.

At the Warrington Borough Court on Saturday morning, before Mr. John Burgess (chairman.), William Thompson, and Ellen Thompson his wife, living at 6, Jones huildinee, were charged with neglecting their five children named Annie (11), Maggie (9), Clara (6), Thomas (5), and William (2) in a manner likely to cause injury to their health. rrisoners pleaded not guilty. Inspector Uphill. N.S.P.C.C., said on the 20th November, about 11.30, he visited the defendants' house, and there saw the five I children hnddled together over a small fire. The chil- dren were very dirty, their hair was matted together, and they had very few garments on. The furniture con- sisted of a table, an old soap box, and a chair. The rooms were in a filthy condition. The only food in the house was one loaf of tread, and on the table there was a bottle centaining beer. The children's bedroom con- tained an old mattress, and the cloteing was quite black. In the middle of the room there was an old butter tub full of filth, and a doe suffering from the mange was lying with the children. The room was very dirty. The defendant w rked on the Ship Canal, and earned £1 per week. On his second visit he watched through the window, and the defendants were quarrelling over some beer, and he concluded they were both drunk. The children were almost always in the market begging, and picking up and eating decayed fruit. He had given the defendants a warning form, but there was never any improvement. P.C. Monks said he went with the Inspector to the defendant's house. The house was in a most filthy condition, and they coubin't Stay in the house above a few minvtes at a time. The children were very dirty, witli vermin about their clothes and heads.— Charles Pastels deposed to seeinZ the children running ellen the streets without shoes or stockings. Elizabeth Turner, wife of James Turner, re- siding at Jones Buildings, said the defendants lived next door to them. She saw the children very often, but she never saw them clean, and they were always poorly clad. She had seen a neighbour giving them fo d two or three times a week.—Dr. Bowden said on the previous evening he went with the Inspector to the defendants, house. He saw four of the five children in charge of police cnnstahle. Ile examined them and found them all very dirty. Diit was caked on their bodies, and their hair Was matted together. He went upstairs and examined the bedding and found it consisted of matting, and was very dirty. There was no other furniture but the beds, and very little downstairs. The children were taken te the Union Workhouse at er he had examined theta, and they now looked a great deal better. Such a feu' at- mosphere as was in the house was likely to injure their health.—School Inspector Jones said he knew the chil- dren well. About eight weeks ago they lived in Buckley- street, but when they removed he lost sight of them. The oldest girl attended school very regularly until then, but the second girl was deaf and dumb.—Defendant said he would get better clothes for the children, but they were all healthy. He gave his wife 17a per week, and she paid 3s. 9,1. rent. She had nevertaeted beer in her life. The magistrates decided to give the defendants another chance, and adjourned the case for a mouth, to see if there was any improvement.
