THE EXAMINER

SATURDAY, DECEMBER 2, 1893.

TRH Marquis of SALISBURY has Lord Salisbury's been this week holding forth at Politica.

some length in South Wales. As every one knows Toryism is in a very bad way in Wales, and its representation in Parliament is exceedingly scanty. We do not think that the speeches of the Marquis will do much to raise the fallen fortunes of kis party. Those speeches cer- tainly effect one purpose very clearly—they dis- play the utter hopelessness and barrenness of the Toryism of to-day in the face of grave political and social questions. Lord SALISBURY does not deny either the existence or the importance of these questions. We have," he says, "to deal with a time in which classes are unhappily divided by deep questions of interest, and a terrible responsibility lies on anyone who has any oppor- tunity, however small, of guiding the public opinion of his time, so to direct it that these classes shall rather come together than go further apart." Seeing that he fully admits, and even emphasises, the need for wise leading, what is the guidance that Lord SALISBURY is prepared to give? or what are the helpful suggestions that he makes ? He lies under some of the "terrible responsibility " he refers to, for he has consider- able influence in "guiding the public opinion of his time." Yet we may read from the opening to the closing sentences of his speeches without find- ing anything in the nature of statesmanlike remedies for the admitted evils he alludes to. It is pure and often merely flippant criticism from be- ginning to end. He is not only sterile of suggestion himself but he has not a good word to say of the proposals that are made by others. It seems quite evident from the tone of his references that the Empl oyers' Liability Bill will meet with only an indifferent reception in the House of Lords, so far at all events as he can control that assembly. It is hard to tell exactly what the noble Marquis wants. It is certain that he is hostile to the mea- sure, but ho does not seem to have quite made up his mind as to what excuse he should put forward ' I for that hostility. He makes the usual cheap and

worthless assertion that he does not object to the principle of the Bill, but he does not make it clear upon what grounds he objects to the i' itself. Does he object to the Bill because it ° not expansive enough, or because it is too exPallo sive ? "I confess," he says, "I am very much e, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN'S opinion in the matter, that I- prefer a Bill that shall deal with accidents gene: rally, without reference to their origin, and deal on the principle of insurance, so that when a rnall suffers under an accident he should have soraa. thing to relieve and console him." If he al in the preferencis and persuades the House ° Lords to follow him, he will materially wide' the scope of the Bill. To have a popular and ben* cial measure made more popular and more hanol. cial by the Peers woald be something to marvel et's But, unfortunately, we may be perfectly suro tha whatever the House of Lords does at the instig.si tion of the Marquis of SALISBURY it not be to extend the benefits this Liabiliti

tb Bill. The real intention of the leader of Conservative party was more clearly revealed wh6 he referred to the question of contracting et/! It is in that direction that he will make his a' tack, and his intention will be not to enlarcre tb pperations of the Bill, but to lessen them. LO SALISBURY, it is needless to add, sees nothing tbl' is good in the Parish Councils Bill. A.:cording tr' him, the result of conferring self-government 00' large section of the community will simply be set class against class, and he seems to be cieit; ready to champion the cause of that class whiell' opposed to the people of the country being a lowed to control their own local affairs as people of the towns now do. Altogether, L°ll SALISBURY is apparently in the mood for bagel ing the inevitable conflict between the peers all the people.

THE House of Lords— A Snub for the Lords. valorous and truly repres five assembly which an Tories tell us will stem the tide of demo(

reform—is already showing unmistakeable siglv of " wobbling." This, in itself, is not ye!' remarkable,for, ever since the era of reform set the House of Lords has always "wobbled " at critical moment and disappointed the hopes of reactionary friends. But lately we have had 5( many confident predictions that the Lords sr°'ll4 bring to naught the whole programme of present Government that any signs of weaknesg°l their part must be truly distressing to their Tot: supporters. It is, however, quite evident 03,11 the occupants of the Gilded Chamber are bel fling to fear that they may take a step too fare will be remembered that a few months ago tb House of Commons passed by a large majoritY ' " betterment " clause in a Bill promoted by tb London County Council, but that the House .° Lords rejected the clause and wrecked the

That rejection was so palpably in the selfish int ests of the ground landlords—the receivers unearned increment—and so much agal

the interests of the public as a whole, and -of residents in large towns in particular, that House of Lords has been censured even by Tel

In order to escape the obloquy that is be heaped upon them some of their lordships hatcl a nice little scheme. They decided to in' the House of Commons to appoint a_ c'

mittee to meet a committee of the lk of Lords and discuss the whole question "betterment." Mr. GLADSTONE'S answer to proposal was straight and to the point.

Government "will not concur in any such prePt; sal" The House of Commons has arrived gt conclusion on that paestion long ago,and expresse it in a definite form, and they did not intend recede from it. As a final slap • the Pima° added, "I do not at all understand why it is tbi/ office of the House of Lords to move us tsP, point committees for which we have no occasl°l/.1 After this unceremonious treatment it is not' e all unlikely that their lordships will come to tbl, conclusion that " stemming the democratic tide is not going to be such an easy and pleasant poi, time as some of them, perhaps, thought it 011 be.

AN article which has boon co Preparing b tdby Sla RCHIBALD A A 8014 for War. u e the December number of "Bloc, wood" shows how easily and speedily a horribi/t destructive war might burst over Europe. shows, too, that the Continental Powers are 05; pecting and preparing for such a war. Thereo. be no other explanation of the fact that tho sian Government has massed nearly a millioo men upon the German frontier. This huge 0114 includes in its ranks, soldiers drawn from neat.prY all the civilised and uncivilised nations and tri% in the Russian empire—Russians, Poles, Jess',4{ Cossacks, Circassians and Tartars. The fate "t Germany if invaded by such a horde would aln3°9o baffle description. It is when thinking of sal' disaster that one can appreciate the motive° the Germans in giving their Kaiser practicsllYl free hand in strengthening the army. It ie understood that when the outbreak—this" 1/711 of giants "—does come, Russia and France be banded together against Germany, Allety:, and Italy. France has increased her army fullest extent her population will permit. enormous as are the combined forces of Roi,, and France, it is not at all unlikely that, W11.,,e the struggle does commence, they may find selves more than out-matched. The attempt 1'1; no doubt, be made to crush Germany before allies can get well into the field, but GermaDY be a hard preliminary nut to crack. Sir Allc",f BALI> ALISON estimates that when its system has been fully developed Germany's alnlito able trained force for war will reach tho enorlo.o number of 4,300,000 men. The combinedar°l,4 of the triple alliance are said to out-number the forces of France and Russia by no fewer thi° half a million.

MR LABOUCHERE keeps up his criticism of Ovi doings of the South African Chartered Conl with relentless pertinacity. He is now anxias re. know what has become of the Matabele who wounded in their encounters with the Cm-013010 forces. He estimates that thare must halm heepA something like two thousand wounded naisr', left on the field after the various encounter."' Considering the destruclive fire of the Mass.„lll guns there must, at all events, have been seve"4s hundreds. / Mr. LABOUCIIRRID asks, what Ilia become of these wounded warriors. There sh‘)ll be a prompt and satisfactory answer to such 6D inquiry as this, though that answer has not cd/ei., been given by the Chartered Company. LAROUCIIRRE almost insinuates that the wolnw.e,4 Matabele have been massacred by the native 13.11,F-; of the Company, but, surely, it may be taken granted that the white men would not permit 5% such atrocity as that to be perpetrated under tm very muzzles of their guns? A singular incident was witnessed on Sunday in the Victoria-St., Veesleyatl Chapel, Douglas, which was crowded. The Rev. T. Rippon, who visited the principal places of amusement in the summer in company, had announced that he would discourse on the mora,s of Douglas, with special reference to the said places of amusement. Prior to his sermon the reverend gentleman intimated that he had re- ceived an anonymous letter, stating in effect that if fw made any false statements he would be there and then publicly challenged in the pulpit The reverend gentleman pointed out that there was a proper time and place for dealing with his asser- tions,quoted a statute against brawling in churches and intimated that if any person conducted himself improperly he might find himself lying on a plank bed. He anticipated opposition from time Press,but . said if he was not dealt fairly with lie would find it his duty to by bare other nets in his possession.ln the course of a vigorous disclurse the reverend gen- tleman condemned LOMB of tie practices he had witnessed at places of amusement, quoted recent utterances of Mr. Spencer Walpole in support of his ' allegations. and contended that what went on was mischievous and injurious to the morals of the place. The reverend gentleman continued his dis- course without interruption.

- BOROUGH BAND BALL. In the Drill Rail, on Monday night the members and friends of the Warrington Borough Band held their first annual ball. There were about 100 pre- sent. • --- The fifth Gilchrist lecture was delivered on Thursday by the Rev. Dt. Da'linger and the Public Hall was filled to repletion. In fact no seats were to be had half-an-hour before the lecture coin- mencen splendid testimony to the popularity of the course. The lecture "Spiders—their work and wisdom" was most beautifully illustrated. The transparencies were designed and drawn by Dr. Hollinger himself and in several by great ingenu;ty, he was enabled to show on the screen, the exact operations he was describing.

Mr. C. Nick3on, the historian of Runcorn, went to much commendable trouble to ascer- tain the correct derivation and meaning of the name Runcorn. New light has been thrown on the subject by a paper entitled " The Mersey as known to the Romans,"which Mr. William ilandley, Chairman of Runcorn Library Committee has discovered in the Picton Library, Liverpool. The paper is written by a well-known antiquarian and historian, Mr. Joseph Bolt, who gives the Anglo-Saxon name of the place as Rumcoren,mean- . ing " the chosen place." It was the place chosen by Queen Ethelflerla,upon which to build her castle. The name has assumed its present form of Runcorn, says the historian, in accordance with that rule of Eng- lish speech by which " m " before a hard "c " becomes " n,"and the "e," being silent has also been discarded.

WARRINGTON ASSESSMENT COMMITTEE. The monthly meeting of the Warrington Assess- ment Committee was held at the Workhouse, yesterday. The Rev. G. F. Roberts presided, and there were present Messrs. \%m. Long, W. Balharry, W. Houghton, J. Houghton, J. Ratcliffe, A. Jolley, Wm. Owen (valuer), J. C. Sutton (clerk). Mr. Wm. Owen, valuer, stated that the Brooks trustees and Samuel Brooks trust, appealed against the assess- ment on certain wOodlands in South worth, Hough- ton, and Burtonwood. The average assessment was at the rate of 325. an acre rateable value. The ap- pelant produced a copy of a case tried before Jus- tices Mellor and Manisty in 1577, where an assess- ment had been reduced in Southwick, by the Quarter Sessions. In this case there had been assessed on woodland property 13s. 3d. for occupa- tion and one shilling for sporting rights. This had been reduced to six shillings for occupation and one shilling for sporting rights. The Justices had pro- nounced the reduction made by the Quarter Ses- sions as correct, and held that a wood was to be assessed as if the land were not a wood, but land let in an unimproved condition. After a considerable amount of discussion the Committee granted the appeal, the reduction being made in accordance with the fulings in the case quoted. This was all the public business.

The same historian also states that Queen Ethel- fieda's husband, founded a Royal burgh for the use ot his wife's family, and to this burgh was given the name of lithwalla,—the town of Ethelfleda. That Royal borough is now known as Thelwall, and It is an interesting fact that on the ordnance map a portion of Runcorn is now shown as being in Thel- wall.

There was a large attendance. The items contributed by M. Tivadar Naehez on his violin were a special feature, Mrs. Egerton Warburton, I Mrs. Philips, Mrs. Egerton Leigh, and Mrs. Mytton Thornycroft took part. VOLUNTEER PRIZE DISTRIBUTION. The annual Concert of the Frodsham Company of th,, 2nd (Earl of Chester) Vit. Cheshire Regiment, was held in the Town Hall, Frodsham, on Wednesday evening, there being a large and appreciative audience. There was an excellent programme, the principal portions being placed under the charge of Mr. Josef Can:or,who, with his quartette company, assisted by Miss Alige Whittaker and the Volunteer Band, monopolised, with the exception of a short interval, the whole of the even- ing. During the interval in the concert the prizes were distributed to the several competitors in the Company, by Mr. James Reynolds, of Fox Hill, who is an ardent admirer and friend of the Company. BREACH OF THE LICENSING ACT.—At Frodsham Petty Sessions on Wednesday before Mr. C. Reynolds, Dr. Creele,and Mr.T.Rigby.--Joseph Faulkner,landlord of the Weaver Tavern, Frodsham Bridge, was summoned for permitting drunkenness on his licensed premises, on October 17th.—Mr. W. B. Churton, Chester, appeared for the prosecution, and Mr. A. Fletcher, Northwich, re- presented the defendant. On Tuesday, October 17th, P.S. Thomas and P.C. Weatherby about nine p.a. were near the Weaver Tavern, when they heard a loud noise going on inside. They went into the taproom and there saw three men, all of whom were drinking. Each had a pint pot before him. Stephen Oaks was very drunk. The landlord said, " I cannot deny he was drunk ; but it was my daughter who served him. lam very sorry, and I hope you will not report me this time. I will see It doss not occur again." The daughter was about 14 years old,—Mr. Fletcher, for the defence, stated that he would be able to show unmistakably that Oaks was not drunk. Oaks was engaged in the Indian Mutiny, and was telling some of his friends his experiences. There was no noise. He had a half-pint of beer, got up with- out difficulty, walked to his house, had his supper, and then took his child to bed. He was not drunk in any degree. After hearing a number of witnesses, the Chairman said the decision of the Court was that they convict the defendant, who would be fined £2 and 16s. 6d. costs.—Stephen Oaks, of Newtown, was summoned for being drunk on the licensed premises of Mr. Faulkner, on October 17th. He was fined 53. in- cluding costs.

W ARRINGTON AMATEUR PHOTOGRAPHIC SOCIETY. At the ordinary monthly meeting of this society, held in the al useuns, Bold-street, on Tuesday even- ing, in the absence of the president, (Mr. T. J. Down), Mr. 11. N. Houghton, presided. A very Interesting lecture and practical demonstration was given by Mr. Herbert Fry of the Paget Prize Plate Co., on " Lantern slides by contact and in the camera." He dealt with the characteristics of good lantern slides, the colour and other conventions,and briefly described the limitations in the manufacture of transparency emulsions, and the uses of the different kinds of Paget plates, slow—rapid, and print-out. He next dealt with the mode of putting in skies, reduction, and local treatment, or what is !better known as " dodging? The lecturer then !dwelt at some length upon the." invidious choice ot ' developers,"and strongly recommended members to acquaint themselves with one maker's plates and formulm, and above all not to use the same devel- oper over and over again as was the case with many amateurs. The lecturet and demonstration proved most instructive, the interest of the members being well sustained by the exhibition of slides illustra- tive of the subject of which Mr. Fry was treating. and the actual development, toning and fixing of several plates, which were handed round for inspec- tion at the several stages of process,and were finally shown in the lantern. The meeting concluded with !the usual votes of tt anks. I SOAKING RAIN PROOF OVERCOATS, yet porous. 555.. C. JACKSON HOLMES, General Outfitter. Market Gate, Warring- tan.--Adrt. TEMPERANCE SUNDAY. —Special services were held at the Baptist Chapel, fligher-lane, on Sunday, when two sermons were preached by the Rev. William Owen, Emmanuel Church, Upper Brook-st., Manchester, on the subject of temperance. In the afternoon Mr. Owen delivered an address to the children. Special collections were made. PRIMITIVE METHODIST ANNIVERSARY.—At the Primitive Methodist Chapel, on Sunday,anniversary services were conducted. The pulpit was occupied morning and even- ing by Mr. S. Hilton, of Leicester. There were large congregations, and the collections were in aid of the Trust Funds. Miss Florence E. Shore presided at the organ. A public tea was held on Monday evening,when about one hundred persons sat down to an excellent repast. The tables were presided over by Mrs. Edwards Mrs. and Miss Jackson, and the Misses Dodds. A meet- me was afterwards held, when the Rev. D.W.Roberts, of Warrington, delivered an address on " As hour with Mark Twain." The chair was occupied by Mr. Hilton.

DIS

IN WARRINGTON.

All the works in Warrington are now in full awing, and it is said that there are even more men employed than there were before the dispute, owing to the large orders which the employers have to get off their hands. The work of relief at the soup kitchens will cease to-day, although the Ladies' Committee will probably be required during the greater part ot the winter.

r WEEKLY MEETING OF THE RELIEF COMMITTEE. A THANKSGIVING MEETING.

The last meeting of the Warrington Relief Committee was held in the Town Hall, Testerday. The Mayor, Alderman E. Greenall, occupied the chair. REPORT OF THE EXECUTIVE COMMITTEE. Mr. Stinley Wilson read the following report of the work of the general committee .—Your Committee in presenting their report on the work done, wish in the first place to put on record their appreciation of the excellent conduct of the men throughout the past 13 weary weeks. The amount of deprivation and suffering, and the sad condition of hun- dreds of families was most deplorable, and baffles all description.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
ORGANIZATION.

It will he remembered that at the town's meeting called by his Worship the Mayor on the 12th September it was universally felt that if the work of relief was to I lie carried out satisfactorily some system of organization • must be adopted. The town was accordingly divided into three districts, each having their own Committee ! (consisting of the clergy and ministers, certain ladies and gentlemen and the managers, or representatives, from the various works in such district) and Honorary Secretary. The amount of relief allowed to adults waa : 1 lb of bread and a pint of soup, or potatoe hash, and half the quantity to children under 12 years of age. The distribution of the weekly tickets for relief was carried out in a most satisfactory manner by the representativeQ from the various works who met at a stated time each Saturday afternoon. By this means alrw,st every per- son receiving a ticket was known to the distributor.

Your Committee have held 12 General Committee meetings and 35 executive meetings. The Mayor proposed and the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton seconded that the report be adopted. LADIES' COMMITTEE REPORT.

Mr. Wilson also read from the Mayoress, the follow- Ariz short report of the work done by the Ladies Committee, formed in cennection with the Executive Committee for the relief of those thrown out of work in Warrington, during the late coal dispute. By the request of the members of the Execu- tive .Comraittee, a committee of ladios was formed shortly after the commencement of the relief work, to visit and inquire into those cases of illness and want, which required more assistance than it was possible to give in the different soup kitchens. The work of the ladies so willingly undertaken,has been no light task,and ' when it is understood that upwards of 320 cases have been visited more than once '1 by one or other of the ladies, it will be seen that their work, in addition to attendance in the soup kiichen each morning, has been most arduous. The cases visited were most of them too sadly evident of the deep poverty into which the people of Warring- ton have been plunged of late, through no fault of their own. Homes, which by great trouble have in ordinary time 3 been kept in comfort, were now found destitute, almost everything having been by degrees parted with. There were cases of illness, with no means of procuring either fire or lights, no bedelothing, and simply no means of existence, excepting by the bread and soup received daily. To cases such as these, after a personal visit, orders had been given for coal tickets, beef tea, gruel, tea and sugar, and hot pots if possible, also for a large namber of milk tickets, these last being abm- lutely necessary to keep alive children, who were found almost dying for want of noiarishment. The amount paid by the Executive Committee for relief given by the Ladies' Committee amounts to £66 is. 6d. The articles of clothing sent by friends in the neighbourhood, and also in response to a letter inserted in the London, Liverpool and Manchester papers, have been found in- valuable, as in nearly all cases visited by the ladies, any article of wearing apparel vu urgently required. The Ladies' Committee have still many cases requiring help now, and for some weeks to come, and think it would be best for their committee to continue working until Christmas, by which time they hoped prospects would be brighter and homes once more fairly comfort- able.

Mr. F. Monks moved and Mr. Smethurst seconded that the report be adopted. Caption Ede!sten moved a vote of thanks to the Ladies Committee, and that they be requested ta con- tinue their labours until a meeting of the subscribers was held on Sffills future date to be selected by the exe- cutive committee. He thought that when tho simple fact that 320 cases had been relieved from extreme sick- ness was mentioned,it was sufficient to show the amount of labour the ladies bad undertaken. The vote was seconded and carried, and the Mayor on behalf of the Mayoress replied. THINGS HEARD AND SEEN

AT WARRINGTON.

It appears to be somewhat of a disparagement to be a teetotaller in the eyes of the dominant tiarty in Warrington to judge from the remarks of Coon— cillorShaw Green at a meeting during the present week. It seems to be all very well to put me* interested in the drink traffic upon the Watch Com- mittee, as has been the case in Warrington,although they, of necessity, have a pers,mal interest in the , work of that Committee, but when Councillor Shaw Green proposed a certain person to be placed ott that Committee the nomination was at once objected to because the person was " a teetotaller." This is siraply scandalous. We shall next hear of Coun- cillors being objected to as members of the Health Committee oecsuee they are sanitarians: or as mem- bers of the Gas Committee because they hold the opinion that we should have better and cheaper gas. In tact if this policy be continued the one qualification for a seat on any committee will be that you should hate an "axe to grind."

It is amusing to note the difference of opinion existing in certain quarters, as to the estimation of the damage done to the Manchester Ship Canal dur- ing the recent flood. The Liverpo )1 papers were loud in their reports as to the alarming destruction caused to the canal, coupling with it the gloomy foreboding that tne canal would not be open" in time." the Manchester papers on the othbr hand were quite re-assuring that the destruction was not very serious, and that the canal opening would not be delayed. It now remains to be seen which of the reports were right. There can be no denying the fact that the canal has suffered to some con- siderable extent, more especially at the banks, which will not be repaired for some time to come, but it is expected that it will not hinder the open- ing for traffic on the day appointed.

One of the most serious effects of the flood to the inhabitants of Walton and surrounding townships, is, that it has deprived them of the use of the tem- porary wooden structure in place of Tom Paine's Bridge, which was so damaged as to render its demolition necessary. The result is that persons coining to Warrington, and vice vrrs.7,are compelled to go round by Stockton Heath. This is owing to the new road proceeding from the Stag Inn, along the river diversion to River-road, not being corn- pleted.Now,th ere is an agreement between the Canal Company and the Cheshire County Council and the Daresbury Highway Board, as to the completion of this road. It is to the effect that the road must be ready for use within six weeks after the demolition of the temporary bridge, under a heavy penalty. As it is very unlikely now that the new road wilt be completed within six weeks from last Saturday, it will be interesting to note whether the fine will be enforced.

The business at the Police Courts is at the present i time very slack, and f you ask for an explanation you will be reminded that Christmas is drawing near, and that the donors to the police court till,are husbanding their resources for the purpose of duly celebrating that auspicious season. Then there will be the usual " bust up," and the business at the Police Courts will go merrily along at perhaps a more accelerated speed than usual.

The influenza fiend is commencing its havoc in Warrington, and as usual it is striking at some of the more prominent residents of the town, The Rev. Owen Watkins has had to cancel all engagements during the week, owing to a very severe attack. By the way, the Wesleyan ministers- are rather unfortunate just at present, for it is only a few weeks ago since the Rev. J. Kirtlan was suddenly struck down with illness, and he has scarcely taken up his work again before his colleague Mr. Watkins is on the sick list. Another victim to the influenza is the Rev. J. W. Varley, who was unable to be pre- sent at the annual meeting of the Social Questions Union on Monday night in consequence. It, is to be devoutly hoped that the enidemic will go no fur- ther, and that the victims at present prostrated by it will very quickly be at their posts of duty again.

The Rev. Cmoir Caine, of London, gave a very en- tertaining and instructive lecture on the North Sea at the Bold-st. Wesleyan schoolroom on Monday night. He rather surprised his hearers by the awful dangers the fishermen had to meet, the collier's lot being in fact much preferable to the fishermen's life. The lecturer was not altogether unknown in Warrington. He has not been so very long in the ministry, and many can remember him when he was a student at Didsbury College, near Manchester. During that period, be occupied the pulpits of the various Wesleyan chapels in the Warrington circuit, and was a very acceptable preacher. His rather unusual Christian, or rather Homan, name was also an aid to the memory of his hearers. HE is at present actively engaged in mis- sion work among the North Sea fishermen, and he has been very successful in that work. His popu- larity with the fishermen will no doubt be largely increased by his taking his share in some of their usual avocations.much as pulling in the nets, sorting the fishes, &c. He enjoys his work immensely, and he has a very high opinion of the fishermen.

A correspondent writes :—By the way, Mr. Editor, it seems sometimes to me,that the Warring- ton Town Council delight to paqi resolutions, and express strong ideas upon certain subjects, for no other purpose than to amuse themselves. Unless it is, as an overworked scribe said, " to hear them- selves talk." At any tate I remember about twelve months ago, or more, the question of allowing cattle fairs to be continued in Winwick-st., was discussed by the " civic fathers." A numser of speeches were made condemning the custom, and a resolution was formed suggesting that some other and more suitable place should be‘provided for the fairs. But on Wednesday and Thursday. one of our most thronged thoroughfares in con rendered pregni eel), dangerous to pedestrians n con sequence of their having to pick their way through an almost impassible crowd of restless cattle. MERLIN.

THE COMMON LOT.

However differently situated as regards social posi- tion or separated as rega.ids location, it is the common lot of all sorts and conditions of men to be liable to various ailments and diseases. All men under these circumstances are, to a certain extent, placed on the same footing. "A fellow-feeling makes us wondrous kind," and nothing interests a sick man more than to hear the experience of others who have suffered from the same complaint as himself. Now, though the names of vari- ous forms of disease are legion, in this country people are subject, more or less, to one of the following form of ailment. 1. Disease of the Digestive organs, such as Indigestion, Flatulence, Headaches, Dyspepsia, Heartburn, Drowsiness, Palpitation, Pm. 2. Diseases of Liver, such as Billiousness, Jaundice, Depressed Spirits, Impure Blood, Melanoholy, Pm. 3. Nervous Diseases, such as Fainting Fits, Neuralgia, Despondency, Nervousness, Nerve Pains, general Weakness, Pic. 4. Diseases ot the Respiratory Organs, such as Com- mon Colds, Asthma, Bronchitis, Spitting of Blood. Shortness of Breath, Pm. Strictly speaking, all these ailments have a common brigin. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

Taß CARRIAGN COMPANY AND Taints HORSES. At the Warrington Borough Court, yesterday, before Alderman Wright (chairman) and Mr. J. 11. Fleming, Richard Gurney,'bus driver for the Warrington Carriage Company. was charged with cruelty to a mare be' work- ing it while in an unfit condition on November 18. Mr. A. Browne appeared to defend in this case and in the next ease, and pleaded not guilty. Inspector Luff, R.S.P.C.A. said that at about 11 o'clock on the 11th November he was at Market Gate, and saw the defend- ant driving a grey mare,attached to a 'bus coming along Sankey-street. The mare was very lame on the near foreleg. On being stopped at the corner of Buttermarket street, defendant said that he was going to change it at the bottom of the street. Witness followed, and when the mare was taken out of the 'bus, he examined it. Be found the pssiorn very hot and tender and sUghtly en- larged. The mare kept lifting her foot off the ground in great pain. He saw the mare the previous day and she was then very lame. On the foot there was a slight corn. —Cross-examined,witness said that he told the manager Mr. Kenrick that the mare was suffering from ringboue. The horse did not flinch when the corn was tapped, but it did when the coronet was pressed.—P.C. HampsOn corroborated the last witness as to the state of the animal on the day in question.—J. Dixon, veterinary surgeon, said that he examined the mare and although he knocked the corn with the knob of a stick, the horse did not flinch, but immediately it was pressed on the coronet, it did so. Ile could not exactly give the cause of the animal's lameness.—Mr. ,Browne said that the mare had been in the company's service for nine years, and there had never been any complaint against it be- fore. It was 14 years of age, and he contended that no horse of that age could throw out ringbones. He did not deny that the horse walked lame on the day in ques- tion, but when it left the stable it was all right. It had a tremendous corn on its foot, and it must have become sore while on its journeys.—Thomas Kenrick, manager, said that the company never allowed their horses to go out lame. He was present when the mare in question went out, and it was all right then. When it came in again it was suffering from pain in the corn, owing to the shoes being rather thin, but, he denied that there was any rinebone.—John Massey, the horse keeper, said that the horse had suffered from a slight lameness on the day previous to the alleged offence, but he had poulticed the foot during the night, and it was all well next morning.—John Robinson, William Clare, and Joseph Taylor, each gave evidence for the defence.—The magistrates considered that the case was not a very serious one, but they were satisfied that the mare was worked when it was lame and inflicted a penalty of Ws. and costs, and allowed the veterinary surgeon's fee.— James Kenrick, the manager of the Carriage Comyany, and John Massey, the horse keeper, were each charged with causing the mare ccmplained of in the last case, to beworked.—Mr. Brcwne objected to the summer's on the ground that each person should have been separ- ately summoned.—The Clerk said that the summons could be amended by striking one oi the defendants names off.—lnspector Luff hereupon agreed upon strik- ing klassey's name out. ,The inspector in his evidence said that when the mare was taken to the stable, he went to Mr. Kenrick, and upon examining the mare de- fendant said that he must confess that one leg was hotter than the,other.—Mr. Browne said that there was no other evidence whatever that the mare was lame when it left the stables in the morning, and that there- fore there could be no case against the defendant.—The magistrates dismissed the case.

MYSTERIOUS DEATH OF A BOLD FARMER, THE INQUEST. The adjourned inquiry into th 3 circumstances attending the death of Thomas Whitfield, Union Bank Farm, Warrington-road, Bold, was held on Tuesday, at the Horns Inn, near Farnworth, before. Mr. S. Brighouse, county coroner. Mr. J. 0. Swift (St. Helens) appeared for Mrs. Whitfield, and In- , spector Pattinson represented the police. The Coroner, in openine; the proceedings, said that last Friday the inquiry was adjourned in order that Dr. O'Neill might make a post mortem examination. The best course would he to call the doctor first, and if he was of opinion that death was from natural causes, there was no need to carry the inquiry further. But if in his opinion death was due to violence, they would have to consider how that violence came about. Dr. O'Neill deposed that he made a post triorteas on the 24th. Externally on the head he found two wounds behind and above the right Par. The higher one was 11 inches long, and the other half an inch. The lower wound had quite healed, and the upper one very nearly so. He next examined internally, and on taking the scalp off he found nothing on the skull. He also removed the skull cap, but found nothing relating to the external wounds. The brain itself outwardly appeared normal to look at, but on the right hemisphere he found a large clot of blood, apparently of recent lodgment. When he found that he was quite satisfied that that- was sufficient of itself to cause death.—The Coroner: Could the clot come about in a natural way?— The Doctor: Oh, yes —The Ceroner : Can you say how it might have come about P—Or. O'Neilt . By an apoplec- tic stroke, or the bursting of a blood vessol.—The Cor- oner : Anything to notice about the other organs ?—Dr. O'Neill: Nothing. Deceased had not been a very healthy man. He came to the conclusion that death was due to apoplexy.—The Coroner: Were you assisted in the post- mortem by any medical man on behalf of Mrs. Whit- field ?—Dr. O'Neill: Yes, Dr. Jacksop.—Cross-examined by Mr. Swift: Witness said that two other doctors were in consultation with him when holding the post-mortem, and they entirely agreed with him. Deceased's heart . was such as indicated an apoplectic person. Dr. Jackson (St. Helens) stated that he was present at the post-mortem on behalf of Mrs. Whitfield. He entirely agreed with Dr. O'Neill's evidence, and had no doubt whatever but that death was due to apoplexy. This was all the evidence called, and in addressing the jury, the Coroner said there could be no doubt that when Mrs. Whitfield was stopped on the last occasion she was willing and desirous of telling that something had happened between her and her husband, but he , thought it would be wrong on his part to allow her to commit herself. Her willingness showed that she had nothing to conceal. If they were satisfied with the evidence of the doctors, they should say that death was due to natural causes, and that whatever Mrs. Whitfield had done it would not be taken into account so long as it had not caused the death of her husband. After two or three minutes' deliberation a verdict of death from natural causes was returned.

RUNCORN AND DISTRICT TEACHERS'

ASSOCIATION.

The annual meeting of the members of this asso- ciation was held in Greenway-road Boys' School,on Saturday evening. The president, Mr. Jordan, occupied the chair, and there was a fair attendance. The treasurer, Miss Kilburn, J.F., submitted the balance sheet, which showed they had in hand .£4 19s. Btd. The document having been audited, it was adopted. Mr. Moulton.- the secretary, then read his report, which stated that the year commenced with a membership of 28, and now stood at 33. Reference was made to the death of Miss Mansell, who for 15 years was head-mistress of the National Girls' School, after which the work of the past year was reviewed. The subscrip- tions of the associaEion, handed in at the Liverpool Conference, to the benevolent and orphanage funds of the Union. amounted to £5 134. 7d. Thanks were accorded to the managers of the schools used by the association during the year, and to the teachers and friends who had helped to make the meetings a success. —The report was unanimously adopted.—The election of officers resulted as follows : —President, Mr. Cookson, Weston Point; vice-president, Mr. W. Epps, Weston; secretary, Mr. T. F. Moulton; assistant secretary. Mr. W. }Leman; treasurer, Miss Kilburn; committee, Messrs Jordan, E. W. Rosser, and G. S. Smith, Misses Clavden and Comerford.—ln accordance with notice of motion it was proposed and decided that the general meeting be held four times a year instead of bi-inonthly, namely in February, June, October and December, the latter to be the annual one.—On the motion of Mr. Jordan, seconded by Mr. Moulton a vote of thanks was accorded to the Runcorn School Board tor their kindness in allowing the use of the room. Votes of thanks to the officers for their services during the pa-t year concluded the business.

The honour of being the " oldest Odd fellow in the world "is claimed by Mr. John Ili 11, of 30, Trafalgar- st.,Derby, who, if he lives till Christmas, will have been a member of the Manchester Unity for 70 years. Born at Alfreton, February 28, 1803, he was initiated into the Prince Edwin Lodge at Chri4mas, 1823,and transferred to the Spring of Love Lod,.ze, Derby, in 1842.

The lato Baron KiMigswater was one of the most remarkable members of the Jewish community in Austria. Ills fortune is estimated at four millions, and his liberality was unbounded. Be gave away hundreds of thousands in larger schemes of charity, while his hall porter had always ten thousand florins in hand as small change for the relief of casual applicants.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, DECEMBER 2, 1893.

LIVERPOOL JUNIOR LEAGUE MATCH. RUNCORN A TWIL V. Sr. ANNE'S PARISH CHURCH.— This match was played on the Forest-road ground, Birkenhead, on Saturday, in wretched weather, rain falling heavily throughout the game. More than usual interest was aroused in the encounter, St. Anne's hav- ing only lost one match in the competition, whilst Run- corn have won all their engagements. The homesters had got together their strongest available team, but Runcorn was short of Butterworth at back, and T. Robinson, three-quarter back. There was a good crowd present when the following players took up their posi• tions :—Runcorn : Lightfoot, full-back ; Myers, Nick- son and W. Gayter, throe-quarter backs; G. Robinson and Hollingsworth, half-backs ; Antrobus, W. Evans,F. Gayter, Welbe, Pendlebury, Meores, Clark, Shaw, and ' Fowler, forwards. St. Anne's : Deakin, full back ;E. I Davies, Weaver, and Beecham, three-quarter backs ; Reyes and Scott,half-backs ; Harris,Basnett, rvlcG )wan, 1 Sherlock, Watts, It. Davies, Blythe, Smith, and Titting- ton, forwards. Harris started. on behalf of the Parish, and a good return was made, play eventually settling at midfield. McGowan and Watts headed a combined rush, and LightfoLt had his kick charged, which almost let the homesters in. Runcorn were hard pressed until some excellent footwork by the forwards changed the venue to more neutral territory. Here Robinson got off cleverly, and a passing movement between the Run- corn backs caused play to be located close to the home lines. From a scrimmage, Hollingsworth passed out to Robinson who gave to Nickson, and the latter threw to W. Uayter, who lost a fine opening by running into the thick Of his opponents. However, the pressure was kept up, and from a forward rush W. Evans succeeded "in scoring, but the kick was not Improved. Following thedrop out the game was hotly cor.tested, both teams showing excellent form. Nothing further was scored up to half time, Antrobus restarted, and quick following up by the Runcorn forwards prevented Deakin from returning. Myers cleverly intercepted a pass intended for Davies, but unfortunately lost possession when he had the line at his mercy. _ Heyes and Scott with some tricky passing gave relief to their side, and Weaver fur- ther improved by an excellent run and kick, which caused the League champions to defend. Myers raised the siege with a superb effort, as upon Weaver screwing ' a kick across the ground intended for Davies, Myers dashed up, and gathering the oval, made a brilliant run dodging man after man, until some ten yards from the line he passed to Hollingsworth, who got over near the corner flag. On appeal the try was disallowed, owing to the latter having been in touch. This seemed somewhat to disconcert the Runcornians, as a mistake by their backs let in E. Davies, who punted over Lightfoot, and Heyes smartly following up completing the try, Harris failing to improve. A hard fought and well-contested game ended in a draw. ST. SHUNS V. Half- time arrived with the score: St. Helens 1 try, Swinton nil. Ia the second half Swinton reversed the irder and did most of the pressing. Jones and Brockbank each got over. Final result :--Swinten 2 tries, St. Helens, 1

RUGBY. WARRINGTON. V. MOSELEY. Saturday was an occasion upon which the public's loyalty to the game was tested. Because as everyone who was out of doors will be aware, there was a bitterly cold wind blowing, and heavy showers of rain falling, all the afternoon. Still the test showed up favourably, for quite the ordinary" gate" braved the obduracy of the elements, and, to put it classically " repaired to the Shrine of Apollo." In other words they repaired to the Causeway ground, and gratified their affection for the "leather propelling art." The visitors are old friends of the home team, but they have not paid them a visit— before Saturday—for four years. At that time they certainly merited the description of being a vigorous combination. Advancing years have not" laid stiffen- fling hands upon the limbs" of the Birmingham men,how- ' ever. Indeed the ancient saying appeared to have been reversed, for it was the opinion of all that Moseley were "smart " and very vigorous. As a matter of fact the merry manner in which they romped about the arena be- came at times highly amusing to the spectators and a little harrassing to the home forces. The gante was en- tirely a forward one, except at such times when at- tempts were made at scoring, and then it was like the farmer who took to drinking claret, "it didn't get any forrader." . During the whole game, both teams had the distinction of scoring nothing. Warrington's ranks had been weakened. The two Bates were both away, with Jolley, at the county match, and other players were away upholding the prestige of their county team by taking their cheers to Bradford. Boscow, however, re- turned to his old post much to the gratification of his admiiers. The following are the teams who responded to the whistle, and assembled on the green and slippery sward : Warrington : Boscow, back ; Carey, Heesom, Law- less, and Potts, three-quarters; Evans (captain) and Clark. half backs ; Fair. Turner, Randles, Berry, Dona- hue, Bailey, Taylor, Hampson, and Thorniley, forwards. Moseley : J. F.Byrne, back ; Patterson, A. E. Rogers, and Frank Bryne, three-quarters; Payne and Cattell, halt backs ;J. H. Rogers Marston, Stevens, Lycett, Bird, Elsworth, Rossiter, Dawes, and Pointuey. Warrington winning the toss, commenced by attacking the railway goal. The doughty men from Moseley commenced by making a vigorous rush upon Warrington territory, but some smart passing by Evans, Boscow, and Carey, soon returned play to a central position. From a free kick awarded Warrington from a foul by Cattell, "Fair" furner judiciously found touch at Moseley's threequarter flag. And then followed a forward rush by Hampson, Berry and Taylor, and just when everybody was expecting to see a score notched, the attack was repelled by Bs rue. A period of forward play now set in, and the visitors demonstrated their capacity ia this direction by pressing Warrington into rather dangerous quarters. Evans and Clatk, however, very timely stepped into the breach, .and saved, though the former a minute later lest a chance by clinging to the oval a minute too long when there was a favourable opening betore him. The aspect of affairs, however, changed at this point, and Warrington getting the ball into rather loose play introduced some passing tactics which for a time made the visitors quite grim with apprehension. But no especial score ensued—it amounted to what the other attempts did, to naught. After this, play gradu- ally worked down to the corner, and again Warrington had cause to be encouraged, until after some splendid passing exhibitions, Lawless made a forward pass to Carey who cleared the line, and the referee said: No, he couldn't grant the pass. This was voted to be hard lines, though it was a correct decision. After getting irritatingly near scoring again, Warrington allowed them- selves to be driven back somewhat. The opportunity was grasped—as well as the ball—by J. H. Rogers, the old international who,made a brilliant run and was only just prevented from scoring by Berry and,Donohue. As it was he succeeded in getting the oval to the three- quarter flag, wnete it received prompt attention at the hands of Boscow. Shortly afterwards Thorniley broke away in line style and brought play into dangerous proximity to the visitors' line: But again Warrington's careful play was frustrated by the unorganized exploits of their opponents, and at half time there had been no score notched. Alter the restart Warrington were placed , in a dangerous position for a time, owing to Carey mull- ing. However the danger was averted, and Turner and Eleesom afterwards made excellent individual attempts. But Byrne proved an invaluable custodian and repeated- ly beat back the loaals. Cattell dribbled well, only to see Boscow at his best, and play hovered between the quarter flags, with hardly an advantage to either side. Eventually Lawless and Potts ran well, and from this point the locals maintained en incessant attack. Clark passed neatly to Evans, who tricked splendidly, and a round of passing, taken part in by Heesom, Lawless, and Carey, saw the latter failing at an easy chance. Heesem made amends with a fine kick, whilst Potts was all but a scorer, the result of a determined run. Taylor, Hampson, and Thorniley next came away from the ruck, only to be pulled up by Byrne, but towards the close,darknes.s was fast creeping on and what play could be discerned was confined to the forwards. Just before the call of time, Rogers made a plucky, though question- able attempt to score by desperately kicking at goal, ten yards from his own posts. But he did not succeed in getting any tangible point scored. RUNCORN V. BIRICENREAD WANDRREFtS.—This specially arranged match was played on the Runcorn ground on Saturday in wretched weather, which rendered the ground unfit for play, and it would have been a wise course to have cancelled the match rather than run the risk of ruining the ground for the whole of the season. There was more interest evinced on the Wanderers' side than amongst the home players. Some difficulty was experienced by Runcorn to get a team. The following players turned out Runcorn: Butterworth, back ; Morris, W. Faulkner, Richardson, and Warder, three- quarter backs; J. Faulkner and S. Abrams, half-backs; Reece, Langley, Taylor, Dolan, Moores, Lightfoot, Wil- liaras, and Farmer, forwards. Birkenhead Wanderers: J. Snape, back; W. Conning, J. Hughes, J. Watson, and C. Kelly, three-quarter backs; E. Hurst and A. Herbert, half-backs • E. Elliott, T. Morgan, B. Murphy, D. Atkin- son, J. Gardiner, W. Bibby, J. Franklin, and F. Ingram, forwards. During a storm of rain Turner started the ball, when the fun at once began, the players having great difficulty in keeping their feet. The ball was kept among the forwards, and now and again some short bits of passing were brought off. During one of the rushes by the Wanderers Gardiner scored, to the great delight of the team and their supporters. The goal kick, how- ever, failed. This was the only peint of interest in the first half, Warder having very hard lines in a couple of penalty kicks, the ball only going a few inches wide of the posts. Changing ends, a re-start was quickly made, and for a time Runcorn penned the Wanderers in their half, and after frequent endeavours to score Abrams got over from a forward rush, Morris being mainly instru- mental in getting the ball up to the line. Darkness leav- ing set in play was almost impossible, and the call of no I aide was a relief to players and spectators alike. Final result :—Runcorn, one try; Wanderers, one try. CIIESHIRS v. OUR LOCAL CIITJRCIIES-W ARRINGTON,

TEMPERANCE SUNDAY.

Temperance Sunday, an institution established by Lady Henry Somerset, was celebrated at the Noncon- formist places of worship on Sunday, special sermons being preached appropriate to the occasion. The pul- pit was oacupied at the Padgate Wesleyan Chapel in the morning by the Rev. G. D. Mason, who said thay could not walk along the streets without being aware of the evils arising Irma the use of intoxicating drinks. The increasing intemperance amongst women and children. was to be deplored. It was very sad to think that nearly all these persons had been scholars in the Sunday schools. The rev, gentleman gave certain statistics from which it appeared that two-thirds, of the youthful population of England, Wales, and Scotland were mem- bers of Sunday schools. In this there was cause for thankfulness. A fact to be deplored, however, was that six out of every seven in our prisons had passed through the Sunday school. How was this to be remedied? Let every scholar in their Sunday schools become a total abstainer. This curse of drink was a great hindrance to the children coming to Christ. Would they then not help to eradicate this hindrance? Let them be an ex- ample to the rising generation in this matter, so that , when Jesus Christ came to pass His judgment on their earthly doings, He might say " Thou hest done well." In the afternoon a special child:en's service was held in , the Sunday school, when addresses were delivered by the Rev. G. D. Mason, Mr. Horrocks, and Mr. Henry Taylor, , of Warrington,

THE P.S.A. AT WYCLIFFE CHURCH. The attendance at the P.S.A. meetings in Wycliffe Church on Sunday afternoons still increases, the mem- bership now numbering 1,058. The chair on Sunday was occupied by Mr. A. Lang who delivered a short address, in which he referred to some of the lessons to be derived from the P.S.A. brotherhood. Mr. Simon of Marple, delivered the address for the afternom, speak- ing on " Righteousness." The address, which was an excellent one was listened to with close attention, and frequently applauded. Mrs. R. H. Pearson sang " Angels ever bright and fair" and also a hymn, the chorus being sung by the audience. The orchestral band connected with the society played several selec- tions, considerably enhancing the musical portion of the service. Mr. W. H. Paytort conducted in the absence of Mr. W. H. Wallington. The Rev. J.Yonge, Mr. A. Birch, and Mr. Pendlebury also took part in the service.

GILCHRIST LECTUitE AT RUNCORN.

DR. DALLINGER ON SPIDERS. The fifth of the course of lectures under the auspices of the Gilchrist Educational Trust was delivered in Runcorn Public Hall, on Thursday evening, by the Rev. W. IL Dallinger, D.S,C., L.L.D., P.R.S., ,The building was crowded in• every part. The lecture was entitled " Spiders, their work and wisdom,"and was profusely illustrated by lantern views, that frequently evoked the enthus- iasm of the large audience. The chair was occupied by the Rev. Thomas Wilkes, who in opening the proceedings referred to the object of those lectures' and mentioned that the permanent educa- tional result of them in Runcorn was to take the form of a technical institute for the town, which he was told was in progress, and in all probability would soon be an , established fact. They believed that the education of the heart and brain was the birthright of every child. I The Elementary Education Act had done much for the advancement of knowledge, so that now working- men's children were receiving what 50 years ago would have been called a liberal education, and the tendency waste give more. Their evening classes in the institution would afford great advantages to all their young people, but let it always be remembered that any practical out- come must be by the hard work and plodding of the individual. Science permitted the true, earnest student to life nature's veil, and view her matchless beauty ; she was ever ready to tell secrets about other worlds, waves of light and water, pedigrees or evolution, and the circulation of the blood. Echoes of these four sup- jects they had had in the previous lectures, and he had pleasure in introducing an ardent admirer of nature, the ' Rev. Dr. Dallinger, to bring before them some interest- ing particulars regarding spioers. (Applause.) Dr. Dellinger, in commencing his lecture, said it was not often the spider became an object of careful study; it was more frequently an object of loathing and disgust. To engage in a rational study of its habits was in the idea of some a study bordering on pursuit worthy of Bed- lam. The strong crushed it, and the weak flew from it; and to all it was an object of contempt. The spider, whether large or small, in the house, or garden, or at large, was a bugbear of the mistress and maid, and in his judgment this might be summed up in one brief sentence—it inherited prejudice. Hi ri desire was to en- able them to judge for themselves that the spider be- longed to a large class to which the men of science had given the name of Arachnida, which was derived from the Greek. The spider had been always and everywhere known by its works. They were objects of deep inter- est to the student in his work, and the spider must in- terest all. Although.the specific function of the spider was to spin, she was not the only spinner of the great class to which she belonged, and the difference between the spider and the silkworm was that the latter spun with its mouth and tha spider by means of external tubes. They would pause at this point to ask what the spider was. The house spider and the garden spider were known even to children, and they con- stantly heard of the red spider of the greenhouse, and the harvest spider of the field, but those two were not spiders at all. The spider was not an insect. Insects and spiders were absolutely unlike each other. The in- sect passed through many a metamorphosis, but the spider was born a spider, and remained a spider to the last. The head of the spider was quite unlike the head of an insect ; its head was soldered to its chest, and its eyes were Movie and not compound, as were the eyes of an insect. The legs of a spider numbered eight, and the insect had only six. The insect was dividi d into three parts: the head, the chest, and the abdomen • but the spider had only two parts. Again, the red spider was one complete body from one end to the other, and that showed the distinction between the mite and the spider. It they took the whole group of spiders they were first separated into two great groups, the sedentary or sitting spiders and the wandering or leaping spiders. The first of these two classes weaved webs or snares, and remained in .them or about them in order to snare their prey. The second were hunters and trappers. The lecturer described in an interesting manner the way in which spiders spun their snares, and overcame the difficulties with which they were oftentimßS confronted. From long observation he had come to the conclusion that the spider was an intel- lectual being up to a certain point.

SUDDEN DEATH OF MR. S. NEEDHAM.

Expressions of surprise were general in Earles- town on Tuesday when it became known that Mr. Samuel Needham, ironmonger, of Queen-street, Earlestown had been found dead in bed between 3 and 4 o'clock that afternoon. Mr. Needham who was 74 years of age was on Friday last working on some property belonging to Mr. T. Forater, when it is supposed that he caught cold. He remained in bed on Saturday, Sunday, and a portion of Monday. lie retired to rest as usual on Monday evening, when he appeared a little better, but he did not get up on Tues- day. About noon his daughter took him some food, hut as he was asleep she did not disturb him. Between 3 and 4 o'clock Mrs. Needham went to reuse him, and I then found he was dead. She summoned medical aid, I but the doctor on arrival said he had been dead about half an hour.—Th,i facts were then communicated to the police, who reported to the Coroner hut he did not consider an inquest, necessary.—Mr. Needham was a well-kriciwn figure in F,arlestown, where ha has resided for something like 35 years, in his early days be- ing employed as a joiner at the Warm Works of the London ano North Western Railway Company. Leav- ing there, he opened an ironmongery business in a shop in Queen-street, in which he was very successful. On the extension of his business he removed to the opposite stde of the street, where ho resided up to the time of his death. For a number of years he was a teacher in the Wesleyan Sunday School, a member of the Wesleyan Society, and was especially known as an earnest visitor of the slck. His life has been a very quiet one, but his labours in connection with the Wesleyun body have been most active and earnest. Be was a man who was well-respected, and there is no doubt such a familiar figure will be much missed. Ile has been twice married and leaves a family of grown up daughters.

THE FASTEST TRAIN IN THE WORLD.

Our American cousins are given we think, at times to "blowing." We here, they think, says a writer in "Transport," and perhaps with equal reason, are given I to assuming our own superiority, even when it has no real existence. Let me, therefore, he says, begin by acknowledging, without qualification, though not with- out regret, that " the fastest train in the world" runs, not in England, but in the United States. It is known as the "Exposition Flyer " ; it leaves New York at 3 p.m. every afternoon, and running via the New York , Central and the Lake Shore and Michigan lines, arrives in Chicago, 984 miles off, at 10 o'clock-11 o'clock by New York time—the following morning. The speed, including stops, of which there are eight advertised, besides numerous slacks and checks for level crossings, taking in water, Azo.., is 45.2 miles per hour throughout. Let us see how this compares with the best speeds else- where. The best Continental long distance train is the Orient express. It takes about zq hours between Paris and Vienna ,or 4i hours more than the American train for 125 miles less distance. Our English distances are so shirt that a comparison on all fours is impossible. But we may put the matter this way with reasonable fairness—London-Perth and New York-Buffalo are practically equivalent distances. The American train takes 8 hours and 40 minutes; our best trains are allowed an entire hour more. Beyond Buffalo—or rather the junction outside it, for the train does not enter the station at all—the speed, which had been 50.8 miles per hour over the New York Central, falls to 48.5 over the Lake Shore. Even so the inclusive

speed throughout is, as I have said, 48.2 miles an hour. When the East and West Coast were racing to Edinburgh in August, 1888, the West Coast trains neatly equalled the New York Central performance, for 400 miles in eight hours is evidently 50 miles per hour. In fAct, it might be argued that the English performance was the better of the two, for the 20 minutes' stop to dine at Preston has no analogue on the other side of the Atlantic. But these trains only ran for a week or two in the height of summer, and no English train since then can dream of challenging a comparison. The present si hours' trains to Edinburgh have an inclusive speed on the West Coast of 47.1 miles an hour. On the East Coast, with eight miles less distance, the time is nearly one mile an hour slower. The Great Northein 4k hours' expresses to Man-

cheater just fail to reach 48 miles an hour. The same I company's best Leeds express just rises above that figure. The Great Western "Cornishman" is below 48, even as between London and Exeter. The new Midland 1.30 p.m. to Scotland, one of the very best trains in the country, is over 47i as between London and Carlisle. But 47 to 48 miles an hour for four or six hours, and 48.2 miles an hour tor 20 hours on end, are two very different things. So we must end the discussion, as we began it, by admitting the American exploit to be abso- lutely unmatched. But it is sufficient to compare sheer speed without the necessary allowances on either side for difficulties of road and weight of train. The West Coast is undoubtedly the harder of the two fast routes to Scotland. As tar as gradients go it would seem also to be somewhat harder than the route . .

of the " Exposition Flyer," though an American migh‘ reasonably enough claim the three or four miles up a bank of one in 70,after a dead stop at Albany as a set-off against Shap, and the 9191 t. summit of the." Batavia divide "as not so much inferior in difficulty to " Beat- tack bank." Even if, however, he waived his claim, he might still satisfy a jury that his own road was the harder of the two, when he gave evidence that all along It there are facing points innumerable, level crossings by the score, miles upon miles run through and across town streets, so that—to take just one instance—the last 16 miles into the Chicago terminus occupy ne less than 36 minutes. There may be room for argument sate the difficulty of the road ; act) the comparative weights there is with a single exception, no doubt whatever. The trains in the race to Edinburgh weighed from 80 to 110 tons. The 10 o'clock Scotch expresses are at present, I believe, limited to go about 160 tons on the West Coast and 140 on the E‘st. The only train among those I have men- tioned which exceeds this figure is the Midland 1•30 to Scotland, which weighs, I calculate. about 180 tons ; but then the Midland uses pilot-engines so freely that I train loads can hardly be reckoned as on the other lines. Much the heaviest train for its speed in Great Britain is the new West Coast dining car train to Scotland, which! weighs no less than 254 tons, as it leaves Eusten. North of Preston, however, this train divides, while south of it the speed is under 44 miles an hour. Its American rival, on the other hand, runs through from end to end as one train dre wn by a single engine. Its weight leavine New York, was, I gather, for the first few days, 320,000tb5. or 143 tons. This was found so much within the power of the engines that a fifth car was attached, bringing the weight up to ever 178 tons. Moreover, for about half the distance in each direction there is a dining car attached, weighing over 42 tons extra, making a total weight of 220 tons, or, say, an ! average of 200 tens for the entire distance. At best,! think, we cannot claim the English performance-254 at 46k miles an hour—to be superior to the American one, 200 tons at 48 15th ; and the American train keeps it up for 20 hours, the English train only for a short 41 houis. I ought to have mentioned that the return tram from Chicago differs only so far that it takes a quartlT of an j hour longer. It leaves at two p.me—three, that is, by the New York time—and reaches New York at 11.15 next morning. Herein lies the great importance of the new departure. Hitherto the best trains took 24 hours on the [road. In other words, a passenger leaving New York on Monday morning was in Chicago on Tuesday morning, but could not, by any means, get back to New York till after business hours on Wednesday. By the " EXposi- tion Flyer "be leaves in the afternoon, when business is , nearly ever, has four hours in Chicago and can be hack Fin Wall-street before midday on the Wednesday. For all , practical purposes he has gained a clear day. Col. Prout evidently expects that further accelerations will, ere long' increase the interval between arrival at and depar- ture from Chicago from four hours to eight. It would not be a greater feat or a more startling change if the newspaper train, leaving King's Cross, at 5.15 a.m., were booked into Edinburgh at 12 45 noon, and the re- turn train, which now leaves at 2.30 were delayed till , 3.40 p.m., continuing to reach King's Cress at 11.10 p.m. as at present. One point more. Several contemporaries are lost in , amazement at the reckless extravagance of space and weight in our new English corridor and dining car trains. Surely,says one of them,we have reached the limit in this respect. Let us see. The East Coast hauls about 165 tons dead weight for 130 passengers, say lf tons each. For the same number of passengers the West Coast hauls 254 tons,or almost two tons apiece. But the American train,weighing 200 tens,has only room for 86 passengers. The deadweight per passenger is therefore 2-1-3 tons. There is,then,a yet deeper deep of possible extravagance yawning before English Companies that venture to begin to Americanise their institutions. Not that I would wish to he understood as implying that these trains are in fact unprofitable. The contrary is,l believe,the case. An average English passenger train earns 4s. per mile. Our two new trains can earn 12e. a mile, and yet leave vacant places, both first-class and third. The American " Flyer" has of course but one class and fare. It is 31 dols. for the single journey, including sleeping berth, as against 28 dols. by the old " Limiteds," and 25 dole., also including betths.by the ordinary trains. Assuming 180 passengers, the "Flyer" would then earn 2,480 dots., lor roughly 103. per mile run. The capital cost of these trains is no doubt considerable, but it spreads out very thin per mile on a train covering 3,000 miles a week. So, on the whole,there seems no reason to doubt that tbe new flyers, both here and in America, will be almost as profitable to the companies as they are convenient to the public. 31

MY NATIVE VALE.

Long years of toil and wand'ring past. Thanks to a friendly gale, My heart's desire is erown'd at last, I see my native vale. Flow gently has the hand of time Dealt with this region fair : Whose lovely scenes and glorious clime Their pristine features wear. Above it smile the same blue skies That met my childish gaze : Still round the shelt'ring hills arise, Unehaned the streamlet plays. The joyous linnet woos his love, The wild bee hums along; And far up, in the blue above, The skylark pours his song. Here still, half hid in blossom'd thorn, With windows quaint and low, I see the house where I was born. A long, long time ago. Ala.! that house, with strangers fill'd, No longer opes to me : And in the fields my fathers till'd New faces all I see. But where are those with whom 1 play'd A merry, careless boy; And where the friends of years more staid Who shared my calmer joy? All, all, alas, are sundered wide BF time's resistless wave: A few in foreign lands reside, But more are in the grave. And while in loneliness I dream. Of vanisled friends and years ; HOW short the span of life doth seem. How full of mists and tears. There is no lasting place on earth, No rest for great or small : And sorrow treads the wake of mirth, Till death envelops all. JAS. H. W. CLINDINNING.

UNITED TEMPERANCE MEETING AT

E A RLESTO WN.

On Thursday evening a United Crusade against liquer traffic was held in the Wesleyan schoolroom under the auspices of the various temperance societies in the town. The Rev. T. R. Mathews pre- sided, and there were present with him on the plat- form Revs. J. Cottam, E. R. Davies, Mr. J. Taylor, O. Pickett and others. There was only a moderate audience. The chairman said theyall knew the cause for which they were met together that night,namely to encourage one another in the good work of waging war against intemperance. They all knew that this sin of intemperance had more willing victims in this world of ours, than any other sin. They all knew very well that intemperance incapacitated a man from doing his duty to his family, how much more his duty to his God' A man under the influence of drink cared not for him- self, his soul, his wife, his children, his Saviour ; in fact he cared for nothing but his own selfishness.—lt was he thought one of the most selfish sins to which mankind was addicted. He thought they were all temperate people in that room, they had not got those they would like to see. He would like to see those old topers who rocked about their streets, but if they did net come,they must go to them. They had a mission to fulfil in that respect, as well as in other res- pects. He asked every individual advocate of temperance young and old,not to leave this business to the ministers of the Gospel. In the next place,he asked them to take up more earnestly this work of temperance, to speak more frequently and lovingly to those whom they knew were victims to this sin. Tney all knew such characters, they could not shut their eyes to seeing them, and they could not avoid coming in contact with them,t hey lived in their ' midst, next door, and possibly in their house, the work had to be done, and the work was brought to them, and laid upon theni. (Applause.) Rev. E. R. Davies Baia they had that night something they could congratulate themselves upon, the progress this question of temperance had made in connection with the christian churches of this country. (Applause.) And they had much to congratulate themselves in this respect, that the christian churches of this country were ' beginu'.ng to work together in a fashion that they had not previously worked. They were not there to de- nounce te e publicans, but it would be better for these gentlemen if they kept a grocer's shop instead of a pub- lic house. All round conviction had already seized hold of people, and they were not there so much for arguing this question, as the time for argument was nearly past, and if people did not by this time understand that two and to made four, they must tell them that it was so, and tell them until they did understand it. There were a great many schemes afloat for dealing with the liquor traffic, but they were not all agreed as te what was best to be done. They should all be agreed upon the ques- tion that Sunday closing for the whole of England was a question that was ripe for settlement, and that it ought to be settled not by a Local Veto Bill, but he believed fairly that they wanted an Imperial measure, by which it shall be passed that public houses for the whole of England should be closed for the whole of the Lord's day. (Applause.) He believed this in the interests of the pub- lican, and the servants of the publican. These servants, and he believed a good many of the publicans wanted Sundayclosing.He did not now speak of publicans, who had tied houses, who could come now and again to their houses to receive what their houses had made, and returning to their home in some quiet resort. Be was speaking about those people who had to work for the publican, and ho held that the bulk of these were ripe upon this question, and would be glad if the bill was carried to-morrow. (Applause.) A can- vass made in Liverpool showed 950 publicans for Sun- ' day closing, and when they said that it v. as demanded from a publican's standpoint they only said What they could vouch for. If it was to be settled speedily, it would be by the temperance party going forward shoul- der to shoulder and pushing matters forward with all the power they possessed, and they would not rest until public-houses were closed almost altogether. (Applause.) The Rev. J. Cottam said he knew that the work they had in hand was so stupendous that some people said they were fools if they expected to lifs this yoke from the necks of the people. But they would take up the the weapons of their welfare, and continue this until crusade, and he trusted they should not be wanting le daring to put their hands to the weapons, and strike blows as opportunities might offer against this great foe ot our land. (Hear, hear.) The trade of the publican was antagonistic to the best interests of Society, arri then, he said, every right-minded citizen should be an- tagonistic to the trade.' He rejoiced in being a rabid teetotaller, and did not know but what they iheuld go in for a little more sledge hammerism in this business. (Applause.) The Rev. E. R. Davies moved " That this meeting while regarding with genial satisfaction the proposals for temperance legislature in various forms which are being advanced, expresses its conviction that public opinion throughout the country is ripe on the question of Sun- day closing, and calls upon all friends of the movement to be firm in pressing the demand for a comprehensive measure which will at once extend the blessings of the entire Sunday closing to the whole cot England." Mr. 0. Pickett seconded, and it was heartily carried. A vote of thanks to the Chairman and speakers closed the meeticg.

ILLNESS OF MR. JOHN BURNS.

The member for Battersea has been laid aside by an illness which threatens to be of a serious character. On Wednesday afternoon, whilst engaged in talking to an official of the vestry in Forthbridge-road, Clapham Com- mon, Mr. John Burns was seized with a sudden illness, and would have fallen to the ground but for the assist- ance of his companion. The hon. member was at once removed to his residence on Lavender-hill, when he was put to bed and medically treated. The precise nature of the disease has not transpired, but it is feared that it is a serious attack of influenza. Mrs. Burns told a reporter on Thursday that her hus- band, feeling unwell on Wednesday, went for a walk across Clapham Common, and was found later lying unconsciously on the ground. It was at first feared that he had been seized with spinal paralysis, but the illness is now believed to be the result of a severe chill. Mrs. Burns added that her husband had overworked himself, and she,should not allow him to leave the house for a week at least. Mr. Burns was slightly better on Thurs- day. db.
