THE DOMESTIC SERVANT PROBLEM.

THE DOMESTIC LAW-GIVER. A case of servants' wages should have come before his Honour Judge Collier at the Liverpool Comity Court on Wednesday morning. The case, however, was settled out of court. It was understood that had the case been heard there would have been some amusing develop- ments, the plaintiff's case including the submission that she was expected to conform to the following rules Any servant who has the good fortune to reside in my house _must comply with the following rules.--By

_ order, Mr. They must be up punctnally at six. have allrneals punctually to time. Must be clean and tidy in their persons, and at their work must not speak unless spoken to. Must not speak at the doors to any of the trades-

people. Must not sing. Mast not wear heavy boots. Must close doors quietly. Must stand meekly while being reproved. Must not answer back. Must be obliging and cheerful. Must be willing to stay in any Sunday or day out when required to, and when asked to do anything to do it quickly and well, and show CO impatience or bad temper, as Mr. -- hates that. Must put up with fault finding and complaining when- ever Mr. __thinks fit to fault find or complain. Mr. -- likes to be called at seven. Takes tea at twenty past seven. Hot towel at twenty to eight, and breakfast at eight prompt, and will have it to the minute, and no nonseuse.—By order, Mr. --

TERRIBLE SCENES IN A RUSSIAN CHURCH. ' CONGREGATION ATTACKED BY COSSACKS. BPIRLIN, Thursday.- A telegram to the " Vossische Zeitung " from Kovno, gives an account of a terrible affair, which is reported to have taken place at Krosche, a small town in the government of Kovno, about thirty miles from the German frontier. The authorities, it is stated, received orders from the Russian Government to close the local Roman Catholic Church, and to prevent this from being done the Roman Catholics assembled in the church and occupied it day and night. Finally, a detachment of troops, led by the Governor, one night forced their way into the building and attacked the occu- pants with drawn swords. Before the building was cleared twenty persons were killed and more than a hun- dred were wounded, The remainder then fled, but were pursued by the Cossacks, and in attempting to escape across a neighbouring river a large number of them were drowned. Some hundreds were taken prisoners, and are to be tried before a court-martial.

The Bed man appears to be in a very bad way, and if reports troin the Dominion are true, future writers Of. backwoods stories will have to invent something more than the plot—they will have to fill in the Indians from imagination, for there will be none left in reality. One Indian town in the northern part of the Province of Quebec has lost every one of its Inhabitants by famine. A MOUNTAIN or CTIEFAH.—Tbs Canadian Mammoth Checse has been shipped by the Dominion Government on board the S.S. Laurentian, of the Allan Line. The American papers state that this gigantic cheese was one of the most attfactive bits at the World's Fair Exhibition Chicago. It weighs something like 24,000 to 25,000 lbs. (nearlv 12 tons), and it has been shipped to Lipton the well-known tea and provision merchant, who will, we nderstand, exhibit It throughout the country.

A DISPUrED see by a letter in last week's paper from J. C. Dalton a va. ,ng statement respecting the late Dean McNeile, of Liverpool. As one who sat under him for many years, I must give a flat contradiction to the statement made. I have a recollection of something taking pia .e respecting a servant who had been tamper- ing with tue master and mistress's children, if not of having one baptised privately by the priest, unknown to the parents, this being related by the Dean—and , once baptised never lost sight of—and he warned mem- bers of the Reformed Catholic Church of England as to the characters of the servants they took into their families. This is the fact of the case, and not the false-1 hood of the Radical papers referred to, if such remarks were made in those papers, which were frequently attacking him,particularly on the education question in the Corvration schools, as to the reading ot the Bible, for the great watchword was scriptural education, a question which caused the late Alderman Robertson Glad- stone to become a Radical during that education contro- versy. The Dean never did attack the Catholic creed -it was the Papal system that heattacked.—l remain yours, AN OLD CHURCHMAN.

-- NR. It. PIERPOINT, M.P., AND THE LOCAL DISTRESS. Sir,— ['he distress in Warrington, during the last 15 weeks or so, has been sad enough to move to pity many true hearted men and women ; on whom the sufforing people have conferred but little public honour compared with that conferred upon Mr. Robert Pierpoint, who kias been made an M.P. But many are finding out that all the cheap talk about being the workingman's friend is like the " barren tree," full of line leaves, alluring the hungry to seek fruit therefrom—but all in vain ! In vain the hungry and starving have looked to find how much Mr. Robert Pierpoint has subscribed to the " Re- lief Fund." Who can tell how much he has given? Baying no family to keep, and some thousands a year to live in, ifl the :present unp ,ralleled distress has only reached his tongue and left his heart unmoved, let the townspeople leave him unhonured in the future. Both political parties are justly much disappointed with him, and will not fail to remind him of the fact that, "Words WI what you should be; deeds what you are." " A friend in need is a friend indeed." Mr. R.P. must take heed !—One on behalf of many, WAITING FOR AN ANSWER.

CHRIST AND THE CATHOLICS. Sir,—As the latter-day champion of Roman Catholic- ism, your correspondent, Mr. J. C. Dalton, endeavours to uphold an inioii•rible religious system by putting his puny opinions against all history, and by drawing upon his imagination for facts. As to Mr. Dalton's argument with Mr. Foulkes, I join with the latter gentleman, inas- much as my reading of history and my travels in Roman Catholic countries had me to the irrisistible conclusion that Roman Catholics will oppress protestants whenever they find their opportunity. But the matter to which I wish to direct the attention of your readers is the ridiculous statement in Mr. J. C. Dalton's letter last week, that the Roman Cotholic reli- gion was made by Christ and the Protestant religion by Luther. If Mr. Dalton cannot produce more reliable statements than this he had better cease to write. I bave sout:ht by every means in my power to acquaint myself with the Roman Catholic Religion, and I unhesi- tatingly say that the Roman Catholic religion as prac- tised now and for hundreds of years back is as much like the religion of Jesus Christ, as revealed in the Bible, as my boot is like one of the statims ot the Cross. There is no referenee in the Bible as to the worship of the Mother of Christ, there is nothing said as to indulgencies for prayer; there is not a word as to the veneration of relics ; there is no mention of auricular confession ; there is nothing said as to priests being a distinct class of the community, as to their having any powers not possessed by any other in- dividuals ; there is no command of celibacy upon the followers of Christ ; nor is there any mention of such a being as the Pope, let alone his having any power from Gcd, not possessed by the meanest of Christ's disciples. Now it was against such doctrines as these, which are advocated even to-day by the Romish Church, that Luther protested. Although a Roman Catholic priest, Luther's study of the Bible led him to see that the Romi,h religion was not the religion established by Jesus Christ, and having vainly attempted to reform the in- iquities perpetrated by the Catholic Church, he left it, and inaugurated the glorious reformation. But to say that Luther established the Protestant religion is simply ridiculous. The Protestant religion—the religion that condemns all the miserable subterfuges that Luther felt Called upon to condemn ; the religion that" protests" against all forms of idolatory, and against any and every system of oppression, was established by the bible, and the bible was certainly not made by Luther. All he did—and his was gloriously successful—was to go to the Bible for his religion, as expounded by Jesus Christ himself, makes every disciple, when confronted with such arrogant systems as Roman Catholicism, what Luther was himself.

"POLEMICAL AMENITIES."

A DISSENTER.

Sir,—One i; aßvsys glad to weLcome--a—peaaemaker , especially if he is sincere. But does it not strike our friend, Mr. Peers, that it is just a little inconsistent to appear to deprecate " Polemical Amenities," and at the Same time to head his deprecating letter by telling Mr. Foulkes and myself that " we bite our thumbs, and we know not why." 'rhen, sir, our oracle goes on to lecture us about " riding the car of logic," &c., and then as an example of his logical acumen, he says "when one is in the mud the other is in the mire." And, to back this up, he says, " they are both right—especially Mr. Foulkes," then he represents Mr. Foulkes "as being in the mire." This reminds me of the saying—" One man is as gcod as another, and a good sight better." So much for the logic of our " Codlin's the friend not Short" peace- maker. Mr. Peers was not able to show his peacemaking powers, without at the same time showing his teeth. First of all he asserts as an historical truth—" an undoubted fact,' he calls it—that the Catholic Church has persecute,l Protestants. Well, Tam only one person, but I will take the liberty of contesting this ass.ertion, which is not" an undoubted tact at all," so far as this . . .

_ discussion is concerned, till it is so proved on independ- ent historical evidence. But I would beg to be permitted to point out to Mr. Peers that this was not the question originally introduced by ma. I have never contended that Catholics have not persecuted Protestants. I readily admit it and deplore it, and condemn it, as wicked, and most repulsive, to every Catholic worthy of the name, and also that the Catholic church never advocated per- secution, but disclaimed the power to persecute. I hope Mr. Peers will see the difference which I am anxious to press home. It is illogical to maintain that, because Catholics have persecuted Protestants, it follows as a necessary conclusion drawn from this fact, that persecu- tion is a tenet of the Catholic religion. For if this wore true of the Catholic religion, it is also true of the Pro- testant religion, inasmuch as it was everywhere estab- lished by persecution. Again, everyone of the Reformers wrote books in defence of persecution. On the contrary, our Canon law stands now as it did in ancient times, and prohibits the conferring of orders on any person guilty of murder, or in any way contributing to the tak- ing away of lite. and in every case where sanguinary punishments have been inflicted for heresy it was the civil authortity which passed sentence and inflicted punishment, and the Catholic church obliged the eccles- iastical judge to plead to the civil pawer for pardon of the culprit. But my purpose in writing you at the present time was more of the present, than the past. .1 am inclined to believe that all persons should endeavour to promote universal toleration, and that no one should suffer any civil disabilities on account of his religious belief. Mr. Peers has shown us in a most unmistakeable way which way the wind blows, by asserting in the most confident way the toleration of Rationalism. Well, now, what about the toleration of Rationalism during the fearful revolution in France? We have just passed the centen- ary of the horrible profanation of the churches of Notre Dame and Salsa Germain des Pres,Paris, which occurred on the 10th of November, 1793. On that day the worship of the Goddess Reason was officially established in the capital, and a frenzied mob escorted with sacrilegious songs and cries to the cathedral, the courtesan who had consented to play the impious part of the revolutionary drama. On that day they enthroned this miserable creature on the very high altar, and two days afterwards the abbey of Saint Germain des Pres was entered and sacked. Ths reliquaries of the Saints were broken in pieces, and the relics of St. Germain and St. Maur pro- faned and destroyed. Nearly all the churches of Paris were subjected to the same fate: and the following day mock processions were organised in most of the quar- ters of Paris. A large number of men and women dressed themselves up in the sacerdotal vestments found in the pillagtd sacristies. In these costumes, and to the chant of the " Carnagnole," varied with grotesque litanies, they carried to the "Convention," and thence to the Mint, all the prc;ious metal seized from ' the " treasuries "of the churches in order to melt them. But enough of this Mr. Peers was ill-advised to write you of " the restraining power of rationalism," with which, says Mr. Peers, " the church of England is shot through and through." To repeat this gentleman again (slightly altered), I would not trust the liberty of a dog to such tryants as the Rationalists, who cry out very loudly for liberty for themselves—but when they eet the power they curtail the liberty of their subjects in such a way that they harass and persecute them in a most shameful manner. No, if condemnation be impartially dealt out, Rationalism will make a very poor show.— Yours, &C. Warrington, 25th N0y.,1893.

.1. C. DALTON.

MONK-STREET AND GOULDEN-SirREET.

Sir,—l noticed a letter in your issue of a fortnight ago, from a " Property Owner" of Monk-street. com- plaining of the proposal of Councillor Plumpton,to have the above-named streets paved and sewered ; and alleg- ing that they did not require it. Now, Sir, I would venture to say, that anyone paying the above-named streets a visit during the wet period we are now hay- ing would find ample reason for this desire of the resi- dents to have the streets dealt with by the " Paving acd Sewerage Committee." I may say, that I have spoken to my landlord about the matter, and he ex- pressed himself as perfectly willing that the streets should be done. Other owners of property in the streets named have concurred with me in the same desire and need, and I believe I express the desire of the majority of the residents of these streets, in urging the Council to have the work proceeded with at once.—Yours, &0., A RESIDENT. Warrington, Nov. 29th, 1893.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0105.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON MUSICAL SOCIETY.

Sir,--The action of the Committee of the Musical Society in electing to give their concert on the 12, Is last in the Drill Hall, has,l understand, not met with unquali- fied approval. I feel that it is to the interest of the , Society, and due to its friends and supporters, to offer as full an explanation as possible thro ugh the medium of your paper, of the Causes compelling the Society to accept the Hall, kindly placed at its disposal by the Colonel and officers of the Volunteer Corps. The Committee has not decided upon giving the con- cert in the hall in question (which, as it stands, is undoubtedly unsuitable) without full consideration of the difficulties entailed, but, there being nc proper COE- cert room or public hall in Warrington, have, as a mat- ter of fact, taken the only place which is available and can be made suitable. The question has been asked : Why not rather go to the Gymnasium ? Acting upon such suggestion the Committee applied for the use of that room but it was declined by the Gymnasium Corn- mittee.

With a view to economising finances the committee propose only two concerts this season—the first of which (on next Tuesday week) is to be given in conjunction with the Runcorn Musical Society provided a room is available in which a platform can be placed capable of holding the enlarged chorus and orchestra. The fees of the principal vocalists and the few profes- sional instrumentalists needed are at a somewhat reduced rate for the two consecutive performances at Warrington and Runcorn, and a saving of money is made by both societies. But this necessitates a room in which a plat- form to hold 250 performers and upwards can be con- structed, and the remaining space for accommodation for the audience would be very small in any room that I know of in Warrington, even were there a chance of securing another, understand that the one principal ground of complaint against the Drill Hall is the absence of any heatin,, apparatus. I can only add that this, too, has been taken into consideration, and a special executive committee of stewards appointed to , make arrangements which are being carried out, and which, I hope, will be successful in rendering the hall comfortably warm. The Musical Society cannot afford to overlook the comfort of its audience (when it gets one) and every effort is being made that can be, to secure warmth and convenience to persons attending the per- formance of " Samson " in the Drill Hall on the 12th of this month. I sincerely trust this letter may explain that the so- ciety is making the best of " Hobson's choice," and hope its friends of past seasons will consider the difficulties under which it labours and—remembering the good work I claim it has done and is still doing,will not fail to continue and increase the support they have extended to it previously, now when it so much needs it. I am, yours faithfully, W. E. ATKIN, Hon. Sec.

Arpley Uue, Bold-st., December Ist, 1893.

THE ETHICS OF INTIMMkTION.

... Sir,—Mr.A.:Brouchemite, to use an Irishism, has " the makings "ot a good ranting demagogue in him. His arguments are adapted to the tastes and understanding, or the want of it, of the gallery. He has a vagrant mind, and will talk of anything except the point in debate— that he will carefully avoid. His last paragraph is very characteristic. I, and Cymro, " are to have a rest and talk to—the parsons and ministers." I have a grammati- cal difficulty in knowing who it is must's talk to their reverences.' I presume it is "AR., as be has now set- tled the vexed question of intimidation by going round it. With Mr. B. this is a question of interest—the in- terest of one section of working-men for whose benefit the eternal principles of justice and equity are to be trampled under foot. In the interest of the working-man himself it cannot be too often reiterated that the right of every man to sell his labour in the best market, and at the best price possible, must ever be rigorously enforced and upheld. Side by side. with this dictum must go the maxim that no interference must be offered to prevent others doing the same. Upon our statute book we have laws to enforc this salutary restraint. But Mr. B. says " the lawyers made Ole laws." Very good ; I care not who made them; my only care is to find them good. I a.n strongly of the opinion that it is in the permanent inter List of no one to be imbued with the Jesuitical idea that it is right to do evil for the apparent benefit of ourselves—of the cause we are advocating. I don't wish to be misunderstood. I am neither an advocate nor an enemy of masters or of miners or simply an observer of the manners and morals of my fellow actors on the stage of life. It may suit the purposes of Mr. Brouchemite and his moonstruck collaborator to misrepresent their opponent, and say he is adverse to the miners getting a fair remuneration for work done. Just the reverse of this is true. Would that every worker could accomplish this happy result—and they might. The talisman for this is combination,sobriety, and thrift. No, Ido not object to the miners getting a living wage, but I do most emphatically object to see other working-men being starved to effect this. I object to see the chemical workers of Widnes marching daily to Prescot Workhouse,some eight miles out and home,V) work for a pitiful Bd. to keep body and soul together ! I object to this and the many ether pitiful sights to be seen as a consequence of this disastrous strife. It will be a bad day for England when she accepts the precept that you may do evil in order that good may come (to your- self.) Anarchy and bomb throwing among innxent persons is the outcome of the doctrines of Mr. B. and his fanatical friend. _ . _

Both Anarchist and the Miners Federation plead that the evil means they use are the only ones they have. This may be so. Personally I disclaim the " right "to injure my neighbour in order to benefit myself. And I do not think any other person, or combination of per- sons possess the " right " which I disclaim.—Yours truly,

JOHN PEERS.

HOME RULE AND THE PROTESTANT MIINORITY.

Sir,—As in my former letter I proved Mr. Dalton to have written inaccuracies, I propose p.)inting out a few more in his letter of last Saturday. He says: Catholic (Papal) nations do not now persecute any one for con- science sake.' This is not true. I take Spain. Mr. D., or your readers, are unable to deny that this i a Romish priest down-trodden nation. Wrli, sir, this very year (on June second) at 01st, a Protestant missions ry,Alex. Lopez Rodriquez was, by the judge of Gerona, con- demned to imprisonment, his offence being • fir writing an arti,,le stating that the wafer is not the body of Christ, and should not,therefore,be worshipped as such.' What value is to he put on Mr. D's assertions without proof? Again, Professor Mivart, a Romanist, has re- cently placed his thoughts in a book ' The Happiness of Hell,' and that book is placed on the index of Prohibited Books. Archbishop Kenrick of St. Louis, Mo., who fought against the decree of the infallibility of the Pope, has been deposed from the Romish Church—see 'English Churchmen' of 2nd inst. Green, published by Morgan and Chase, London, page 29. IVe have just had a good object lesson of what Home Rule might be, at the scene at a Board of Guardians at Tulla, Ireland, over the election of a medical officer, when at the head of the table a • local character' sang popular songs for an hour and a half. 'f he chairman could not maintain , rder, although lforty police were at his command, fie did not dare Ito use his authority (see Liverpool • Courier' of 22nd llost.). Professor T. Maguire, of T.C.D.,an Irish Roman Catholic, says Home Rule means the boycotting and massacre of the loyalists.' Mr. D. does not ciesire to fol- low me. Why? Because he knows I am stating the truth, ' The lip of truth shall be establish( d for ever : but a lying tongue is but for a moment.' The do- eument was inspected by three men of irreproachable character, viz., Dr. John Cumming, Sir J. He.an Max— well, and Admiral Vernon Flarcourt, and on page 88 (Paris edition, 1664) of the Pontifical then submitted by order of Cardinal Wiseman, the clause was found. Now, Mr. D., if you are an honourable controversialist, you must either withdraw or pay a fee to the Warrington Infirmary. You ask me to get proof of your bare assertion, re Hugh McNeile, late Dean of Ripon. I will give you a little of his medicine, preached in St. Andrew's Church, Liverpool, on January 27, 1836 :—' Undoubtedly, in- stances of persecution by Protestants may be cited, but they. were acts in defence of, not in accordance with, the acknowledged principles of the men who committed them. They were the lingering leaven of tht ir Popish education: the last vibrations of a pendulum which their newly-acquired Protestant principles had not yet been able to arrest. But what Protestants,we ask, arepledged

to persecute? What Protestants are bound by oatli,t) a creed which fastens upon them such principles as those decreed by the Council of Lateran?' On another occa- sion he said the Romish apostacy was 'Politically tyran- nical, ecclesiastically schismatical, theologically hereti- cal, and socially immoral.' Leo XIII. placed, in 1887, Ruin Lasseri's four gospels in the prohibited list of forbidden books, although he gave it his approval the year previous. Does not this leok like persecution,even to Papists? THE EXAMINER, SATURDAN, DECEMBER 2, 1893.

NOTES ON

GAMES AND

ATHLETICS.

RUGBY FOOTBALL.

The elements do not appear to have been looking down upon local football with much favour lately. Many of the local junior teams, the members of which have per- haps not been taking lessons in swimming, have had to take a rest until their fields are a little leas suitable for aquatic performances, and more serviceable for exposi- tions of the game.

Despite this gloomy glumness of the elements, how- ever, the supporters of our senior club did not show any abatement in their enthusiasm on Saturday. They assembled in very strong force to witness the perform- ance with Moseley, and apparently they treated with majestic contempt the roar of the boisterous wind, the patter of the pelting rain, and the invisible antics of that detestable influenza microbe, which is said to be working overtime among us. In return they were rewarded by seeing both teams work their hardest with- out even scoring a try. The game itself was not particu- larly edifying from a scientific point of view, but it was very, very, lively. It was a game played between the forwards, and if it did not result in any scoring, it resulted in making the spectators laugh considerably, and become not a little excited. The two Bates and Jolley were of course away helping the county team. Boscow, however, made his appearance again at full back, and was heartily welcomed back to his old place of honour.

The visiting custodian, Byrne, was in capital cornii- tion, and the only close escapes which Warrington had of being scored against, were the outcome of brilliant efforts made by Byrne. Boscow showed up very well in comparison, but of course he was kept pretty well idle as there was no back play. Moseley men introduced an element of vigour which bordered very muck on rough- ness, but they were successfully checkmated in this direction by Donohue, Berry, and Hampson. Towards the close, play became very quiet, and the only exciting incident which occurred was a desperate attempt made by the old international, Rogers, to kick a goal for I Moseley. against a strong wind, when he was only ten yards from his own team's goal posts.

The match of the day, was of course, that between Lancashire and Yorkshire. Those who saw it were simply enthusiastic in their commendation of the be- haviour or the two Bates and Jolley. Although the d nighty Tykes claimed victory for their own, they did not confiscate all the credit. The Bates, so long as they received fair support, were admitted to be more than equal to the opposing half-backs, and their smart and dexterous combination was quite a source of surprise to the spectators. Lancashire's only try was the outcome of their efforts. John Bate took up his old position near the scrimmages and was ever on the alert to pass on the oval to Bro. "Smack," who was always in waiting near an open course. Jolley made another great impression, both on the public and on the county officials. For the work he did everyone voted him a "jolly good fellow," and I a:n confidently informed—l had it from two con- temporaries—that his chances fcr Northern honours are particularly bright.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GYMNASIUM.

Matters have been a trifle quiet for some time at the institution in Palmyra Square. However, I am in a position to state that providing epidemics and such like intruders keep away from the town during tie coming season, we shall hear more from the home of the gymnasts. The adult classes, both male and female,are very strong now, but I regret to say that the juniors are not turning up very well.

By the way, the building has been most 'artistically renovated—and what is better, time bare, blank, chilly looking walls have at last been painted and decorated. The work has been most generously done gratis by Mr. John Hesketh, in whom the institution has a capital friend and patron. lam glad this renovation has been carried out, because, as everyone must admit, it will have a beneficial influence on the " displays," if any are given during the coming season. I suppose this subj,-.ct of entertainment, is a sort of " bone of contention " among the Committee. It is said that public displays are being objected to by some of them. I only hope this is untrue and that no such foolish idea has got abroad among the Committee.

Of course the institution is going to make acother at- tempt to secure the N. P. 11.8. 100 guineas shieH. The "eight" have not been completely selected. J.Podmore, J. E. Warren, G. Vernon, A. Vernon, J. Mottram, F. Bennion, and A. Dickens, have been selected again. This leaves one vacancy which will be filled up by either Joseph Vernon, G. Whitehouse, or G. Clay.

The exercises to be gone through in the competition are: vaulting, jumping and rope climbing. This la a sec- tion which has pot met with any approval at the hands of true gymnasts. Neither of the three exercises brings into play the capacities of a thoroughly skilled athlete. I think, however, it is a selection which will not hold good in many more competitions. However, from what I can gather, Mr. J. Kinsley, is energetic and painstak- ing director of the " Gym," s training his men with so much trouble that they are sure to make a high bid for the " trophy."

===== 0002601_18931202_art0110.txt | included=true | included =====
ANTECEDENTS.

Our sketch this week is of one upon whom a consider- able amount of attention has latterly been concentrated, especiallyin connection with Lancashire county matches. It is the speedy and brilliant forward, " Jim " Jolley. Ile is another old member of that defunct Padgate team which served as a training institution for most of our present day local football celebrities. Upon the amalga- mation of Padgate with Warrington, in 1881 Mr. Jolley of course adopted the jersey of the latter. At first ho was relegated to the" A" team, but after a few weeks play his sterling qualities as a player had made their mark, and he was placed in the first team, in which he has figured up to the present time. His place in the team was at three-quarter back, and this place he retained until about four years ago,when he became a "forward." Upon his performances with the Warrington team it is not necessary to dilate as they are sufficiently well- known to spectators of the wikter pastime on the Wilder- spool enclosure. Jolley's brilliant performonce against Wakefield Trinity in 1889, will be remembered still by many. Warrington had always suffered &feat from the Yorkshiremen but on this occasion Warrington were determined to win, and they did so, for in the last few minutes of the game Jolley droeped a goal which was the winning point. He also distinguished himself at Dewsbury-, and Halifax.

County honours may be said to have been showered on Jolley during the last three seasons, and it is some- thing to be able to say that he has played for the county oftener other than any other Warrington player. During his three seasons play in the county, Jolley has played against almost every county team, in- cluding Yorkshire, Cheshire, Durham, Westmoreland, Cumberland. Glamorganshire,Devonshire,Somersetshire, and a,,ainst Cambridge University. This in no way comprises all his important and distinguished engage- ments out of Warrington. About six years since he played three-quarter back for West Lancashire against Westmoreland. It should also be stated that he has played twice in the "Stripes v.Whites" matches. His position in the County team has always been in the forwards, and at Bradford in the match last Saturday against Yerkshire, spectators are said by the " Athletic News " to have been continually asking " whoever that energetic, bald- headed chap was," thus showing that his play was very prominent as well as the place where his curls ought to be.

There is good reason to believe that Jolley will be chosen for the Lancashire v. Cheshire match, and rum- our has it that he is likely to be selected to-day for the North te.im against the South. If this is so, and I shall be surprised if it isn't, it will be another feather in the cap of the Warrington team, for it will be the first instance in which a Warrington player has been chosen for such an important match. That the Warrington Football Club is proud of such a player may be best judged by the fact that he was recently presented with a valuable silver cup. It bears the following inscription " 1892-3—Presented by Mr. Percy Davies, president of the Warrington Football Club to James Jolley, in hon- our of his playing for Lancashire County and of his consistent play for his club."

Nor does " Jim's " laurels end as a football player. He has in his possession a considerable number of prizes, many being of the silversmith's art, being the rewards for his skill as a runner, billiard player, and bowler. They have been won in and around Warrington. As a runner at 220 yards, he has won many cups and other prizes, and in billiards and bowls he is a noted player.

At St. Ilelens on Saturday, Mr. Brighouse held in- quests on the body of William Brown, killed by a fall of a roof in Whitecross Colliery on Friday ; on the body of James Finnigan, an old man, who was blown down by the gale on the previous Saturday, and died from in- jury to the skull in the hospital ; and on the body of Bridget Brady, the illegitimate daughter of A widow living in Mount-street, who was fatally sealdid by fal- ling from a chair to the grate, and knocking a kettle of boiling water over her. Verdicts of " Accidental I death" were returned.

TUE San) CANAL FLOOD. A MAN DIT6WNED.

The high winds and heavy rains of last week had ata unfortunate effect upon the ship canal, on Saturday. The Mersey becoming swollen, burst, its banks in two places, and, its surplus waters finding their way into the ship canal, soon aug- mented the few feet of water then in the channel to an overflow. The rumour of this, found its way t 3 Warrington in an exaggerated form on Sunday morning. Tie result was, that during the afternoon and evening hundreds of spectators flocked to Latchford and S'oekton Heath to wit- ness the flood. It was then seen that the sandy portion of the banks, above the ordinary water line, had been in many places swept away by the rushing current. The company's officials were, however, everywhere actively engaged avert- ing possible damter from weak points. And, al- though damage was done, it was not so serious as was at first supposed, . _ .

The waters entered the canal through the Irwell at ' M4nchester and at the Mersey at Irlam, where they ' poured in a vast volume into the channel and rushed towards the sea. This commenced on Saturday . morning. All day the water continued to pour in until ' at length, by mid-day, there was more than a depth of ' 26 feet of water. And this was where the serious part of the misfortune commenced. So powerful was the rush of water as its vast volume swirled seawards, that it soon became necessary to open the sluice gates at Latchford and allow it to enter the length between that place and Runcorn. Near the latter place the length was dammed off from the opened length from Eastbam, by a large hank of earth. Hard by are another set of sluice gates,which unfortunately were not quite finished. Con- sequently where the water was admitted at Latchford it soon rose in this long basin until towards evening it was seen that ualess an abatement took place the increasing weight of water at the dam end would have serious re- sults. No abatement occurred. Had the sluice gates been completed the water could have flowed harm- lessly through them into the river and simply left in the canal the necessary 26 feet of water. But as it was, the flood from Irlarn continued, and the banks all along the waterway commenced to overflow. The water had risen to a great height. And now the over burdened enci by the dam began to fail. The canal at Handles Creek is separated from the Mersey by only a thin neck of land. This was burst with the terrible force of water, at a late hour on Saturday night,and the surfeited canal began to tumble its surplus water headlong into the turbid current of the Mersey, which was swollen by a high fresh. The breach caused was 50 yards long and 20 yards wide. All the next day water found an easy egress here, and soon the channel was at the requisite depth of 26 feet. By this time the engineers had commenced to fill the breach with earth and rock, a task which was completed by Tuesday. This, however, was not the only damage done in the neighbourhood, a temporary bridge at Acton Grange,known as" Rob's " Bridge was also swept away and completely wrecked. However, this was shortly to be demolished. The overflow of the banks also flooded many acres of meadow land, whilst the people who live in the huts at Moore, rose on Sunday morning to find themselves inhabiting a small island, with no means of access to the mainland. This was accompanied by the loss of a life Four men endeavoured to leave the huts on a raft,when the planks which had been unsecurely made fast, separated, and the four men were plunged into the water. Three of them succeeded in reaching a place of safety, hut the other, Thomas Hewitt, 26, was drowned. It was also rumoured that the coal shipping wharf, at Warrington, belonging to Messrs. Evans and Co. had been wrecked. This, however, was found to be untrue. A little damage had been done to the work, but it was nothing of a serious character. , An " Examiner " reporter,upon making investigations , on Monday, learned from several engineers that the damage done was very small indeed,and had been much overstated. The accident was one which cannot happen again. Indeed, had it not been for the dam at Runcorn , no flood would have occurred,tor the water would have been allowed to flow out at the Eastham lock gates. The reporter approached the breach opposite Fidler's Ferry by the river. The filling up of the rent was then going on rapidly. But it was very obvious that the accident , might have been mare serious. The" lay" of the canal , here is below the level of the river, and the ground which divides it from the river is of a soft,peaty nature. Upon this a huge bank of soil had been tip.,ed, and its weight had already dislodged the solidity of the peat,and , swollen it out some yards into the river bed. Therefore, had the masonry at the sides of the canal in this loca-

lity not been laid with the utmost care, it is very possible the catastrophe would have been infinitely worse.

AN EXCITING ADVEN MEE.

_ The temporary bodge which was erected as a substi- tute for Tom Paine's Bridge, on the Chester road, near Stockton Heath, was considerably damaged, and was rendered so unsafe that its demolition was commenced on Sunday. A well known stall holder in the Warring- ton market, named Mrs. Massey, met with a startling adventure in crossing this bridge at midnight on Satur- day. She was driving h. me accompanied by her family. None of the party had heard of the hod. Consequenty when, upon reaching the centre of the bridge they were unpleasantly surprised to feel the structure swinging and groaning,and at hearing the water rushing with a hoarse roar only a few feet beneath them. However, the party reached the other side in safety, although it is quite possible that had they been an hour later another and more serious result might have attended the adven- ture.

TEIE RAFT FATALITY.

- An inquest was held at the Red Lion, Moore, before Mr. Dobson, deputy coroner, on Tuesday afternoon, on the body of Thomas Hewitt, who was drowned near Bob's Bridge, on Sunday during the flood in the Canal. Thomas Hewitt, Connah's Quay, labourer, identified the body of the deceased as that of his son Thomas, who was a labourer on the Ship Canal. He had been at Moore seven or eight months, and was 24 years of age. John Mitchell, living at the Ship Canal huts, Moore,and employed as a foreman on the canal, said that deceased lodelicl with him. About half-past eight on Sunday morn- ing he made a raft to fetch two men who had been on the other side of Moss-lane since two o'clock in the morn- ing. He and a man made the raft, which was composed of planks nailed together with six-inch spik s. They did not reach the men they wanted to rescue. After going a little way the water got on one end of the rat t and three of the four men who were on it slipped off into the flood. Deceased was one of the three. 'the raft drifted away, carrying the fourth man with it. One of the men got out himself, and another was rescued by witness and others holding a long pole out to him. They could not reach deceased, and none of them could swim. He gradually sank, and . his body was recovered at twelve o'clock. Ihe accident happened eight minutes after the men got on the raft. The fourth man afttrwards got to the side. This con- cluded the evidence, and the jury returned a verdict of "Accidental death."

===== 0002601_18931202_art0113.txt | included=true | included =====
OFFICIAL REPORT,

The Ship Canal Company suppliad the following account of the disaster :—" The Ship Canal is now full of water from end to end. The depth of water in the section from Latchford to Runcorn, nearly eight miles in length, on Saturday was 14ft. The heavy rain on Saturday enabled the full depth of 26ft. to be obtained by Sunday morn- ing, but as the Mersey contiaued to rise rapidly the canal level rose 14ft. higher than required. The canal had been constructed to allow of a rise of tide in exc ss of its orlinary depth, but this large extra rise caused some grass meadows near Moore to he overflowed. The sluices at Mode Wheel, Barton, and Irwell,passed off the fiord easily without a comparatively small rise, but the Latchford sluices could not be kept open after the canal was tilled, as the dam at Runcorn had not been removed. The dam was required to keep the canal full from Eastham to Runcorn, and could not be taken away until the canal was full. On Sunday morning,under the super- intendence of Mr.E Leader Williams, chief engineer, mad Mr. H. Abernethy, resident engineer, the dam was cut through, and the great volume of extra water in the canal allowed to flow through the tidal opening at Run- con, into the river Mersey, leaving the canal at its proper depth. The only damage d.ne by the flood is to the soil on some portions of the slopes, and to a tempor- ary bridge, known as Tern Paine's Bridge, Wildersponl. This bridge was to have been removed shortly, as the permanent swing bridge is now completed, and the road traffic diverted over it. The temporary bridge was con- sidered unsafe to be used after the flood, and is now being taken down. The dams at Latchford and Runcorn will be dredged out, and the flood waters will then pass through the Latchford sluices along the canal to the Mersey at Runcorn without any exceptional rise, as the existing river's channel through Warrington, which has been the only outlet for floods, will be available as here- tofore."

A CONTRADICTION. The following telegram has been received from Mr. Grimk6 Drayton, the managing director of Richard Evans and Co., Limited, Haydoek, concerning the damage done to their coal shipping lay-by at Warring- ton. He save :—" I have just returned from the lay-by and find that, practically speaking, no serious damage has been done. The lay-by itself is completed, and the rest of the work will be finished quitP as soon as we ex- pected, and we shall be able tl load coals into vessels on the opening of the canal, or, if necessary, before that date."

===== 0002601_18931202_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN.

On Saturday there was a depth of about 15 feet in the Ship Canal above the dam at Old Quay, Runcorn. It was not expected that the required depth of 26 feet would be attained until Wednesday or Thursday. Those who visited the place on Sunday morning were surprised to find thirty feet of water in the cutting and still rapidly rising. At one o'clock there was a depth of about 43 feet, and fears were entertained that this unexpected and extraordinary weight of water might break down the dam. It was therefore determined to make a breach in the obstruction and relieve the pressure. The water was now everflowing the banks of the Canal at Wigg's works. A signalman's box was washed down, and the watchman's house was flooded. A portion of the soft soillembankrnent above the works gave way and the

water flooded the marsh. A breach about four feet wide I was made in the dam to the level of the water, and soon the water was rushing over like a cataract. In about half-an-hour the water had cleared an opening 20 feet wide and went splashing and dashing down the lower stretch of the Canal like a mill race. A portion of the river embankment was dislodged, and some waggons, loaded with stone which were standing on the weir, were overturned and the stones scattered. A num- ber of flats were moored alongside the Canal, about 200 yards below, and fears were at one time enter- tained for their safety, but the moorings held good. By half-past four only about a quarter of the dam remained and the water had subsided so rapidly that it would not be more than 29 or 30 feet in depth. It was then seen that the embanlsments had stood the strain remarkably well. The face of one part of the embank- ment, just above the turn bridge, is the natural red sandstone rock, and this was scarred and worn by the scouring action of the water. Of course this portion was expected to be always above high water mark. The weir above Widnes Lock was of great advantage in carrying off the rushing water into the river.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
INSPECTION BY THE ENGINEERS.

On Wednesday, an inspection of the canal between Warrington and Runcorn was made by the directors, who were accompanied by Mr. Leader Williams, chief engineer, Mr.Abernethy,engineer of the section, and Mr. Jones, the agent of the Runcorn length. Some of the party traversed the distance on the overland route from Tom Pains's bridge, whilst the remainder got aboard the Ashton, one of the Canal steamers, at Twenty-step Bridge, and sailed as far as the Runcorn Docks, without coming across any obstacle. The various places at which damage had been done by the floods were carefully in- spected. It was found that the breach at Randle's Creek had been closed, and the repairs fully conapleted. The gap in the Old Quay bank, which proved to be 71t. above the ordinary water level, was being filled in. The directors, after the carrful inspection, expressed surpisle at the comparatively little damage caused under such extraordinary circumstances as those prevailing during Saturday night and Sunday. They were delighted to find that the works had so satisfactorily withstood such a severe test. It is intended to commence dredging opera- tions for the removal of the remainder of the dam at Old Quay Runcorn, at once.

SCHOOLS COMMITTES.

This Committee recommanded that the usual Christ- mas dinner be given to the officials and children. They also reported that outside iron stair cases had also been fixed and fitted for No. 1 home by Mr. Watkinson, Sal- ford, and were considered satisfactory by the Commit- tee. They recommended that homes Nog. 2, 3 and 4, be fitted up in a similar manner, at a cost of £B4 according to tender.—Mr. Wm. Houghton moved the adoption of the minutes. He said that the iron stair cases were very valuable in caie of an outbreak of fire, because it only required throwing up the window, and the children could be out in a very short time.—Mr. Muir said that he considered the steps to be too steep,and if there was a scramble the children would tumble down the stairs.— Mr. Shaw seconded the adoption of the minutes.—The Chairman said he would not move an amendment to the minutes, but he was of opinion that a shoot was of far more use than stair cases.—Mr. Houghton pointed out that it would require an official at the top a.d bottom of the shoot, and in case of fire, they could not always be obtained. He thought that the stair cases were both cheaper and more serviceable than a shoot. With regard to Mr. Muir's complaint about the steepness of the stairs, they were put up by practical men who knew what was required to be done.—After some further discussion,the minutes were passed unanimot TEACHERS SALARIES.

TEMPERANCE MEETING AT BOLD-ST.

WESLEYAN SCHOOLS.

A. temperance meeting of members of the Warring- ton Wesleyan Circuit was held in Bold-st. Wesleyan Schoolroom on Wednesday night. There was only a moderate attendance. The chairman was Coun.. Shaw Green, and he was supported on the platform by the Rev. J. Gunnell, and Messrs. S. B. Stringer and Pigot. The chairman apologised for the ab- sence of the Rev. G. D. Mason' as he had to fill an appointment of the Rev. Owen Watkins, owing to that gentleman's serious illness. He was glad to be present at that circuit temperance meeting. He could well remember when the temperance move- ment was not so popular as it was now. But ROW they had not only had they circuit temperance meetings, hut the command of the conference that in every pulpit in Methodism on the last Sunday in November, the principles of total abstinence be enforced upon the people. He remembered the discreditable scenes which took place in Warrington when the militia assembled from time to time, and were quartered in the public houses throughout the town. On the first day of their com- ing, the town was a perfect pi ndemonium,and it was not sate for respectable people to pass through Horsemarket at. into Winwick-st. All that had changed. A wonderfu change had come over the habits of the people, although he did not think that the total amount of liquor drunk was very much less than formerly. It was drunk in a more quiet and orderly fashion than it used to be. There had been many reforms, for the welfare of the working classes, hut he thought that there was no reform more necessary than the reform of the drink traffic. One of the defects in his opinion, in the:strength of the temper- ance party in ‘Varringtnn was disunited action. They required one grand united organisation that would take in the whole town like one man. They saw the whole of the licensed victuallers combining together for the protection of their interests, and if that was so, they, who had the welfare of the people of this country at heart, should unite with a determination to deal with the traffiC. 4 number of men were elected at the municipal election who were directly interested in the drink traffic, and only two temperance men were re- turned, and by the way it was rather remarkable that those two should be the only outspoken temperance men in the Council. (Applause.) When they were discuss- ing the question as to which men should be upon the Watch Committee, he took the opportunity of suggest- ing a name to be put on that Committee. A member of the other side instantly jumped up and asked if his friend was an abstainer. He (Nlr. Green) wished to know what that had to do with the question, and besides a temperance man was an infinitely better man for the post, because those who want to maintain the drink traffic had a direct, personal, monetary interest in doing ao, while the temperance party only wished to lift up the social and moral condition of the people, and yet they were told that they were not fit to have the care of the Watch Committee and of the police of this borough. He noticed with much interest the Social Questions Union which had been formed in the borough. It was a union of the religious and Christian community in the town. He could not understand a portion of the Cliririan com- munity standing aside from that trli' In which was only to promote Christian fellowship and the welfare of the inhabitants if the town, Ile also wished the sanitary state of the borough to be improved. He knew most of the slums in the town, and he could tell them that they would not find a well-behaved, orderly, temperance man living in the shims. The degradation, misery and wretcheJness there, caused the people to live in drunken and almost immoral conditions. During the recent con- test, lie visited one or two of those places, and he was extremely glad to get out again. His opponents were there in the greatest possible numbers. He did not find that to be so in the best parts of the town. In conclusion he hoped that they would do their utmost to promote the cause of temperance in their own homes, in the town and in the country at large.—Mr. S. B. stringer alluded to the progress which the temperance movement had made during the last few years. He urged his hearers to put away mere sentimentalism, and to work in the cause of temperance in Parliamentary, Count? Council and municipal elections.—The Rev. J, Gunnell next gave ,an address upon the effects of alcohol upon the human body.—A number of recitations were given by members of the hand of hr and the meeting closed with a vote

. nope, of thanks to the Chairman.

WARRINGTON DEBATING SOCIETY.

===== 0002601_18931202_art0121.txt | included=true | included =====
MINING ROYALTIES.

The weekly meeting of the Warrington Debating Society was held on Tuesday evening, under the presidency of Mr. Thomas Morris, F.R.G.S Mr. T. J. Hulse read an admirable paper on" Mining Roy- alties." He said that before the final report of the Royal Commission on Mining Royalties, which was published at the beginning of this year, no correct estimate as to the average amount paid per ton for royalty could be formed, and the figures given in the report were based on the out-turn of minerals for 1889. The output of coal in the United King- dom in 1889 was 176.916,724 tons, on which was paid in royalties and wayleaves £4,210,289 which aver- aged sfd. per con. The output of ironstone arid iron ore was 14,548,105 tons, royalties and wayleaves paid £575,903, average 41. per ton. On other metals such as copper, tin, lead, Ste., £87,068 were paid in royalties. The royalties varied greatly, especially as regarded coal, some royalties on coal being as low as 20. whilst others ran as high as is. 3d. per ton. This arose from various reasons. The mines might be in the case of the higher royalties well situated geographically, the coal good,and easy to obtain, whilst in the case of those mines which paid the lesser royalties the conditions were adverse. He had come across many cases of hardship which the mineral tenant had to suffer through unforeseen adverse conditions, and which as the law stood to-day did not tend to the economical development of this important Industry. He referred especially to overpaid mine rents which were estimated at many thousands of pounds. With regard to foreign competition in the Iron and steel trade, to which industries our existence as a manufac- turing In tion depended, and to which we owed our present high position in the world, 30 years ago we pro- duced as much iron as all the other countries of the world combined. To-day, America' at one time the best cus- tomer of this country had developed her wonderful natural resources with surprising rapidity,and continued to do so to such an extent that not only could she com- pete with us in independent markets, but could actually beat us on our own ground. Germany, since the Franco- German war had been steadily improving her resources and manufactures, and we had now to note with alarm that during the past ten years her export trade in iron and steel had devoloped at a rate out of all proportion to this country. Whilst we from 1880 to 1892 showed an increase of 70 per cent; Germany had achieved during the same period a net growth of nearly, or quite,4oo per cent. Spain was also developing her mining industries, both as regarded coal and iron and other ores, and would in future have to be reckoned with as a competitor, as she appeared to be attacking markets which had hitherto been exclusively English. Other countries were of course tending in the same direction, and must naturally have a proportional effect upon us. Royalties could be reduced by either a percentage, or an equal reduction. He believed that the owner in the first instance would benefit providing a reduction took place. The owner would retain his hold until forced by competition, or bad times, to permit the consumer to share the benefit of it by a reduction of prices. The wage earner would not be taken into consideration, and it would only be by prompt action on the part of the miners' organisations that the whole or part of the reduction might be intercepted before it reached the consumer. However, the margin out of which wages and profits came would be greater, and consequently the benefit to the miner would t alp. the form of continuation of employment, and possibly the avoidance of a reduc- tion in wages. Mr. Hulse contended that the total abolition of royalties, from an economic point of view, would not be an advantlge, as the least favourably situated mines would be stopped, and would be a loss on the part of the workers in such mines. He also gave figures showing that royalties had little or no effect upon our coal export trade, as they were still increasing. But turning to our production of iron ore, the essayist pointed out that in 1880 we produced 18 million tons, and in 1890 131 millions, showing a decrease of over four millions in the decade. He thought it was worthy of note that we imported over three million tons of iron ore in 1880, and nearly five millions in 1890. The explana- tion of our decreased production, and increased impor- tation was to be found in the fact that about ten years ago a new method of manufacture came into existence known as the basic process, which was especially advan- tageous to Germany, and it was calculated that in 1890 over 28 per cent, of pig iron made in the United King- dom was made from imported ore. He was convinced that if the minerals of this country were made natio, al property, and thus brought directly under the cords* and management of the State, the mines could ~e worked more economically than at present. Difficulties that now existed as regarded wayleaves,boundaries and drainage would be swept away. The safety, comfort, and well being of the miners would be more carefully attended to than in present circumstances. If the State owned and worked the minerals, the State could fix the price cf coal as it now fixed the price of a postage stamp. As coal would he produced mote cheaply, the consumer would be benefittel bya reduction in price,and a stimulus, under such conditions must inevitably be given to the industries of the country. A discussion ensued in which the Chairman, Messrs. Nield, Plinston, Caldecott, Bunch, and E. J. Hall took part.

SALE OF WORK AT ST. PETER'S CHURCH.

Owing to the increasing demands upon the ener- gies of the Vicar of St. Peter's parish, the Rev. B. Percival, it has been found necessary to have a cur- ate to assist him in his duties. To raise the balance required to meet this extra expense, a sale of work , was held in the St. Peter's schoolroem commencing lon Thursday afternoon. There were four stalls laden with useful and ornamental articles; also a refreshment stall and book stall, and in the middle of the room was a flower stall very artistically set out. The stall holders were as follows :—Vicara,ce stall, Mrs. Percival, Mrs. Richardson, Mrs. Pearson, and Miss Smith ; No. 2 stall, Mrs. Carleton ; No. 3 stall, Mrs. Green, and Miss Balshaw ; No. 4 stall, MIL Balmforth, and Mrs. Savage; book stall, Rev. J. Cote; flower stall, the Misses Green; refreshment stall, Miss Edgar, Misses Brookes, and Miss Madeley. The above ladies in addition to Mrs. Stanley, Mrs. Edgar, Mrs. Cox, and Miss Heaton formed the ladies' committee. The sale was opened by Mrs. Bea moot, and there were also present, the Rector (Rev. F. NV. Willis) and Mrs. Willis, Rev. B. Percival (vicar) and Mrs. Percival, Rev. E. C. E. and Mrs. Carleton, Rev. H. V. and Mrs. Bigot, Rev. Morley Stevenson, Rev. J. Cole, Captain Buckton, Messrs. J. Roberts, R. Norman, Vera Davies, and Blea se.

The Rev. B. PERCIVAL in introducing Mrs. Beamont said that he would take the opportunity of saying some- I thing about the work of the parish. The members of St. Peter's Church used to meet, in that room, but now there was a spacious building where they could worship to the honour and glory of God. Since the time that the church had been built, it was considered desirable that the vicar of the parish should have someone to assist hii.. in the spiritual oversight of the parish, and when the size of the parish was considered, they would ve that it was not unreasonable. The parish was the second largest in the town, and it was certainly among the poorest. There were over 9,000 people in the parish and they were almost entirely workpeople. It would therefore be seen that for one to work the parish I thoroughly and properly would be impossible. Hence it was almost absolutely necessary for them to have a! curate. They were opening the sale of work that after- noon in order that funds might be raised to assist in keeping a curate for the parish. His friend, Mr. Cole, was very anxious to do his best, and those who were in the parish could testify to the energy with which he had taken up his work, and he would make a valuable worker. They required and hoped to raise at the sale of work £lOO clear, which would enable them to do with- out another sale for two years. When the fund w first starred, the late Mr. Sharpe who was a great friend of the parish, promised' £5O a year, but since he was called away. his subscription had been lost. It now required £5O or £6O 'a year to be raised in addition to the amount of subscriptions already coming in from different subscribers. Mrs. Reatuont had been a very great friend to the parish and he was very glad she had consented to open the sale. Mrs. BEAMONT said that she was glad to take the opportunity of congratulating the parishioners upon having such an excellent pastor, and she was glad that Mrs. Percival would very shortly settle in their midst in the vicarage now ready for their reception. He was sure that they would . all help and support them to the uttermost, in all good works for the benefit of the parish She hoped that they would do their best to empty the stalls that afternoon. The sale then commenced and business was pretty L. brisk. An entertainment was given during the evening by the day school teachers connected with St. Peter's • School, which was largely attended. The total receipts taken during the day amounted to a little over £45 11.. There was no formal opening of the sale of work yester- day, and it was only moderately attended. The Bank- street Mission minstrels gave an entertainment in one ot the classrooms last night. PE NKETH.

LIVERPOOL FLAT SUNK IN THE MERSEY. —The flat Crow, belonging to Mr. George fiooton, Liverpool, was sunk on Saturday midnight in the Mersey opposite Car- ter's-house Bridge, three miles from Fiddler's Ferry. She was being towed to Warrington by the steamer Ada when she ran into the Marsh and staved in her bows. She was laden with manure. The crews were saved hy boarding the steamer just before their boat went down.

Urban Authority for the said District, HEREBY GIVE NoTICE, in pursuance of the said Act, that the Audit of the said Accounts will be made at the Time and Place so appointed as aforesaid ; that any Ratepayer or Owner nf property in the said District may be present at the Audit, and may make any objection to the said Accounts before the Auditor; and that a Copy of the said Accounts duly made up and balanced, together with all the rate books, account books, deed;, contracts, accounts, vouchers, and receipts mentioned or referred to in such Accounts, will ,be deposited in our Offive in Pepper-st., Lymm, on TUESDAY, the 12th day of December instant, and will be open thereat, during office hours until the said day of ' Audit, to the inspection of all persons interested, who will be at liberty to take conies of or extracts from the same without fee or reward.
